                                  Hooray, the price type is here

    Now let's consider another example of pricing. Prices can be
    changed quite frequently - on a supply and demand basis, or
    because the items are near EOL (End of Line), or because simply
    you get a better deal from suppliers. In such case, it's not technically
    promotions, but you still want to show the discounted price to
    the customer. It's pretty strong encourage to say "Was $1200, only
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                    128

    $1000" now. This is a pretty way to make customers decide to buy.

1 public static IPriceValue GetPreviousPrice(this IPriceSer\

2 vice priceService, ContentReference contentLink, MarketId\

3 marketId, Currency currency)

4{

5     var contentRepository = ServiceLocator.Current.GetIns\

6 tance<IContentRepository>();

7     var entryContent = contentRepository.Get<EntryContent\

8 Base>(contentLink);

9     var catalogKey = new CatalogKey(new Guid(entryContent\

10 .ApplicationId), entryContent.Code);

11    priceFilter.Currencies = new List<Currency>() { curre\

12 ncy };

13    var prices = priceService.GetCatalogEntryPrices(catal\

14 ogKey);

15    var previousPrice = prices.FirstOrDefault(p => p.Mark\

16 etId == marketId

17          && p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.ValidUnt\

18 il.HasValue && p.ValidUntil.Value < DateTime.UtcNow);

19    var currentPrice = prices.FirstOrDefault(p => p.Marke\

20 tId == marketId

21          && p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.ValidFro\

22 m < DateTime.UtcNow && (!p.ValidUntil.HasValue || p.Valid\

23 Until.Value > DateTime.UtcNow));

24    if (previousPrice.UnitPrice.Amount < currentPrice.Uni\

25 tPrice.Amount)

26    {

27          return previousPrice;

28    }

29    return currentPrice;

30 }

    The last line is a small but important thing to check - if the price
    was increased, it's better to silently ignore the difference. We only
    show information if the item is cheaper.
  Chapter 5: Inventories

Inventory is another "detachable" system in Optimizely Commerce,
and yet, just as important. In real world scenario, Inventories are
usually updated from external systems, such as ERP. So they were
designed with "interface" approach in mind - they are easy to decou-
ple from the system and be replaced by your own implementation.
Let's examine two concepts: warehouse and inventory.

                   Warehouse

Warehouse is the place you store the goods, it might be used as a
distribution center, or a pickup location.

    Editing a warehouse in Commerce Manager

Same as entries, the code of warehouses are supposed to

be unique, and it's used to map between warehouse and

inventories. You'll work with Warehouse via IWarehouse

and IWarehouseRepository. There is nothing special about

these interfaces - you can check the documentation

at  http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-
Chapter 5: Inventories                              130

library/?documentId=commerce/9/8BCAE8CB             and

http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-li-

brary/?documentId=commerce/9/4A181B5. Normally, you will

work with Mediachase.Commerce.Inventory.Warehouse, the default

implementation of IWarhouse.

This, however, comes with a special note. When you use
IWarehouseInventory to get a warehouse, you get an IWarehouse
implemented by ReadOnlyWarehouse - an internal class. Yes, the
instance you get back is read-only and you can't directly edit it.
You will need to create a "writable" clone of the instance, like this

1 var warehouse = warehouseRepository.Get("warehouseCode");
2 var writable = new Warehouse(warehouse);
3 //edit writable warehouse
4 warehouseRepository.Save(warehouse);

    an alternative is to call CreateWriteableClone as IWarehouse imple-
    ments IReadonly<T> from Commerce 12:

1 var warehouse = warehouseRepository.Get("warehouseCode");
2 var writable = warehouse.CreateWriteableClone();
3 //edit writable warehouse
4 warehouseRepository.Save(warehouse);

    Those two methods are equipvalent as the latter uses the former
    internally.

                Extending warehouse

A question that is frequently asked is that if a warehouse can be ex-
tended to contain more information (say, open hours for example).
The default implementation does not allow that, you would need
to implement IWarehouse and IWarehouseRepository youself, and
effectively saving the data to a different table. Fortunately both two
interfaces are quite simple and straightforward to implement.
Chapter 5: Inventories  131

                    Inventory

While you will hardly work with warehouse API:s, you are sup-
posed to work with inventories, a lot. Unlike warehouses which are
rarely changed, the inventories are adjusted continuously - when a
customer places and order, when a supply comes to a warehouse,
etc.

If you are new to Commerce, perhaps it's worth spending some-
times to explain the terminologies with the inventory: the inventory
is for a SKU at a specific warehouse:

    · InStockQuantity is the quantity you have in stock - it's
       the amount of goods that your warehouse can fulfill, minus
       ReservedQuantity. For example, if you have 100 items in store,
       but the reserved level set to 5, you can only sell 95 items.

    · ReorderMinQuantity is the minimum quantity for re-order.
       Once you your instock quantity reaches this level, the entry
      will be shown in the "Low stock quantity" report.

    · AllowBackorder is a flag indicating if you allow customers
       to order this item, even if it's currently out of stock, if
       the BackorderAvailabilityDate, if set, is passed. The or-
       ders will be fulfilled later when the goods arrive. The num-
       ber of items ordered by back-order will be added up to
       BackorderQuantity.

    · AllowPreorder is a flag indicating if you allow customers
       to order this item before the day it's available, if the
       PreorderAvailabilityDate, if set, is passed. For example,
       if you set AllowPreorder to true, then your customer still
       to wait until PreorderAvailabilityDate to pre-order. The
       order will be fulfilled later - the number of items ordered by
       pre-order will be added up to PreorderQuantity.

    · InventoryTrackingStatus is a flag indicating if the work-
       flows - should care about the inventory at all. If this is set to
       Disabled, then the workflows won't check if there is any item
Chapter 5: Inventories  132

      left in store when a customer adds it to cart, or won't adjust
      the inventory when they place the order. Don't be confused
      with the TrackInventory property of a VariationContent -
      even thought they serves the same purpose. TrackInventory
      works on SKU level, so if it is set to false, it will over-
      ride InventoryTrackingStatus. InventoryTrackingStatus is
      at warehouse level.

Then how the "old" inventory system work?

If you use a vanilla Episerver Commerce system - meaning
you don't have any customization to the workflows, then
usually you work around IWarehouseInventoryService - a
pretty big interface which provides CRUD operations to
IWarehouseInventory, as you can find its full documentation
[^here](http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-
library/?documentId=commerce/9/56465654).

    · You can use IWarehouseInventoryService.List overloads to
      load the information for entries shown to customers. Depends
      on your website's policies, you might want to show some-
      thing like "7 available", "Less than 5 left", etc. However, it's
      generally a good practice to at least indicate if an item is out
      of stock or not, and if it should be refilled soon.

    · When a customer add an item to cart, you'll normally call
      CartValidate workflow to do some checks (applying pro-
      motions etc.), and the system will verify if the inventories
      have enough quantity for the goods. In cases when the
      quantity can't be fulfilled, the quantity might be reduced,
      or the item even removed from the cart. This is done in
      CheckInventoryActivity.

These steps are not changed in the new system. However the next
step was.
Chapter 5: Inventories  133

    · When the customer finally places an order, the value of
      InStockQuantity (and in some cases, PreorderQuantity, if
      it's a pre-order, or BackorderQuantity, or a combination of
      InStockQuantity and BackorderQuantity) will be updated
      to subtract (or add, in case the warehouse is refilled.) the
      quantity has been ordered.

This design works to a point. It serves basic cases quite well,
however, it has some major limitations which led to the new
inventory system:

    · The quantities are set to a calculated value, such as if it was 10
      and a customer places an order of 2, then it's set to 8. Normally
      this is non-issue, but if your item is being sold like hot cake
      (and of course, you would want it to be), it's possible that two
      or more customers are placing orders at the same time - then
      all the threads will try to set the InStockQuantity to 8. This
      is a race condition and causes serious problems.

    · After a purchase order is placed, there is no way to know the
      status of inventory - is it ready for packaging, is it released to
      shipping, how should we do if a customer cancels the order -
      should we simply revert back the change because the goods
      were still on the shelf, or should we record them as outlet
      because they were packaged and were ready for shipping?
      Once the changes are made, there are no real way to "revert"
      them.

Then comes the new inventory system - which is built around
IInventoryService - it's supposed to be smarter and more flexible
than the old one. It was introduced in Episerver Commerce 7.19,
but was sitting there silently until 8.7, when it was fully inte-
grated into the workflows. (Before that, the new APIs were there
but the new features were not used by any parts of the system.
Any inventory operations were done in a "compatible layer", the
    Chapter 5: Inventories                         134

    calls to IWarehouseInventoryService are forwarded to work with
    IInventoryService internally.)

    The new inventory system is much more sophisticated and power-
    ful than the old one, so we will examine it piece by piece. Firstly,
    by the basic "InventoryRecord" class to represent a record of an
    inventory:

    This is more or less map to IWarehouseInventory, which some
    differences. It's the easiest to explain is to look at how an
    InventoryRecord is converted to an IWarehouseInventory:

1     private IWarehouseInventory RecordToInventory(Guid ap\

2 plicationId, InventoryRecord source)

3     {

4         return WarehouseInventory()

5         {

6            CatalogKey = new CatalogKey(applicationId, so\

7 urce.CatalogEntryCode),

8            WarehouseCode = source.WarehouseCode,

9            InStockQuantity = source.PurchaseAvailableQua\
    Chapter 5: Inventories                               137

10 ntity + source.AdditionalQuantity,

11              ReservedQuantity = source.AdditionalQuantity,

12              ReorderMinQuantity = source.ReorderMinQuantit\

13 y,

14              PreorderQuantity = source.PreorderAvailableQu\

15 antity,

16              BackorderQuantity = source.BackorderAvailable\

17 Quantity,

18              AllowBackorder = source.BackorderAvailableQua\

19 ntity > 0,

20              AllowPreorder = source.PreorderAvailableUtc >\

21 new DateTime(1900, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, DateTimeKind.Utc),

22              InventoryStatus = source.IsTracked ? Inventor\

23 yTrackingStatus.Enabled : InventoryTrackingStatus.Disable\

24 d,

25              PreorderAvailabilityDate = source.PreorderAva\

26 ilableUtc,

27              BackorderAvailabilityDate = source.BackorderA\

28 vailableUtc

29          };

30     }

    We can see that:

       · InventoryTrackingStatus only has Enabled and Disabled
         now as the other flag - Ignored is obsolete. This is mapped
         to IsTracked.

       · InStockQuantity equals to PurchaseAvailableQuantity
         plus AdditionalQuantity, while AdditionalQuantity is the
         ReservedQuantity.

       · AllowBackOrder will be true if BackorderAvailableQuantity
         is greater than 0, and AllowPreorder will be true if
         PreorderAvailableUtc is greater than a specific time (the
         beginning of 20th century, only 116 years ago)
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                          138

    So the way data is stored is changed, but not much. The biggest
    change is in how it works.

       When the new inventory system was introduced,
       the data from old system was migrated to new one,
       and the old interface was moved to a new proxy
       class (WarehouseInventoryProxy), and the old class
       WarehouseInventoryServiceDatabase was obsoleted -
       and it's not just obsolete in term of API:s - the old class
       works with the old way of storing data and that means
       those data are not used anywhere else. This is why
       you should avoid using concrete class from Episerver
       unless absolutely necessary. Sticking with interfaces
       is always the safer way.

    The new APIs are built around the InventoryRecord as we see
    above, and IIventoryService interface. As we mentioned earlier,
    IInventoryService is a quite big interface, as it provides everything
    you needs to work with the inventory system. Here's just a peak of
    it:

1 public interface IInventoryService

2   {

3      /// <summary>

4      /// Gets a single inventory record for a catalog \

5 entry in a warehouse

6      /// </summary>

7      InventoryRecord Get(string catalogEntryCode, stri\

8 ng warehouseCode);

9

10     /// <summary>

11     /// Lists all inventory records.

12     /// </summary>

13     IList<InventoryRecord> List();

14
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                           139

15           /// <summary>

16           /// Lists all inventory records of requested entr\

17 y codes.

18           /// </summary>

19           IList<InventoryRecord> QueryByEntry(IEnumerable<s\

20 tring> catalogEntryCodes);

21

22           /// <summary>

23           /// Lists all inventory records matching requeste\

24 d entry codes

25           /// sort by the entry and warehouse codes, with p\

26 aging.

28           IList<InventoryRecord> QueryByEntry(IEnumerable<s\

29 tring> catalogEntryCodes, int offset, int count, out int \

30 totalCount);

31           ...

32  }

             I'll say it again: this interface has same issue as
             IPriceService - it's too thick. It should have been
             a small interface with just CRUD operations. Other
             methods, such as query inventories by warehouse,
             should have been in a separate interface, or to exten-
             sion methods.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                140

It's very important to know that all methods are
not equal in term of performance. Some methods,
such as List() is very heavy (it returns all records
in your inventory, which can be a million or even
more), and should be avoided. Other methods, such
as QueryByPartialKeys, are not effectively cached.
If you want to have best performance, stick with
QueryByEntry. Unlike prices where prices are not
frequently updated, the inventories can be changed
continuously (when a customer places an order, it's
a change already), so effective and smart caching is
very important. Actually QueryByEntry is the most
common operation you'll ever use. In general, you
should never use List(). I think introducing it was
a bad idea.

              Commerce 10.7 drastically improved the caching of
              inventory system. Previously, every change to inven-
              tory system (And that can be a lot, during a campaign
              when everyone is placing order), the entire inventory
              cache is invalidated. From 10.7, only related invento-
              ries cache will be invalidated. While this might seem
              to be a small change to be mentioned, it was my
              contribution, which I am proud of. The moral of the
              story: use the latest version you can. There will be
              improvements that benefit you, and your business.

Now let's try a simple example. When a customer visits a product
detail page, you probably want to display the number of item in
stock. (You might also to do the same with product listing pages).
Depends on your business requirements, you might want to display
the exact number in stock, or you only display it when the stock is
low (one trick to urge the customer to buy!).
Chapter 5: Inventories  141

1 var inventoryRecords = _inventoryService.QueryByEntry(new\
2 [] { entryCode });
3 var inStock = inventoryRecords.Sum(i => i.PurchaseAvailab\
4 leQuantity);

    Simple, eh?

                   Yes, it is a simplified sample. In real case you would
                   check and display information if the item is available
                   for pre-order, or if it is currently out of stock, but
                   can be back-ordered and the shipment will be shipped
                   as soon as possible. You can get everything from the
                   collection of IInventoryRecord you have.

                Checking inventories.

When an item is added to cart, normally you would like to check if it
is still in stock. If it is not, but it can be pre-ordered, or back-ordered.
And it's not just that. For a SKU you can set the MinQuantity and
MaxQuantity, which decides how many items of that SKU can be
in a cart - you can either set the MinQuantity to decide that the
customer by at least by batch of 3, or 4 - or set the MaxQuantity so
they can't buy more than that, or a combination of both.

You would not want to do all of those works yourself - that will be a
hell of if. Luckily for us, that is something the framework provides
out of the box. We will look in more details in chapter about order,
but let's have a glimpse here.

To do such task, you can go old-fashioned with workflow
(ActivityFlow) like this:
Chapter 5: Inventories  142

 1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(Cart, OrderGroupWor\
 2 kflowManager.CartValidateWorkflowName);

     It's the ValidateItemsActivity which does the work (and some
     extra).
     Or you can be "modern" and go with the new abstraction APIs:

 1 cart.ValidateOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validatio\
 2 nIssues.AddValidationIssues(item, issue), _lineItemValida\
 3 tor);
 4 cart.UpdatePlacedPriceOrRemoveLineItems(_customerContext.\
 5 GetContactById(cart.CustomerId), (item, issue) => validat\
 6 ionIssues.AddValidationIssues(item, issue), _placedPriceP\
 7 rocessor);
 8 cart.UpdateInventoryOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => va\
 9 lidationIssues.AddValidationIssues(item, issue), _invento\
10 ryProcessor);

     These three lines are equivalent with the old fashioned way above,
     but to check and validate inventory, you just need the last line.
     However, checking for inventory is just the first part of solving
     problem. When an order is placed, or when it's handled, you have
     to let the inventory system know how you want to handle stocks.
     And that can be done by "requests".

                       Into requests

Checking inventories is just one part of the problems. As a devel-
oper, you will have to adjust the inventories. In the old system, as
we know, it's "Setting the value". In the new inventory system, it's
"requesting a change". Whenever you need to adjust quantities, you
send a request to the system - you no longer tell how should the
system do it, you only tell the system what you want to do and it
    Chapter 5: Inventories                         143

    will figure out how to do it (remember, it's supposed to be smarter!).
    This information is contained in an InventoryRequest:

1 public class InventoryRequest

2{

    This class is quite simple and easy to follow: RequestDateUtc is of
    course the time of the request in Utc, while Items is a collection
    of items being requested (customer might be placing an order
    with several items, and you only have to send one request for the
    entire order. Context is an extra object for customization - this
    property is not used by Episerver itself, but it is reserved for custom
    implementation of inventory system. So what do we have in an
    InventoryRequestItem?

    Each InventoryRequestItem maps to a LineItem in an order, and
    beyond that, there are several properties which might interest you:

      · ItemIndex: So when we get the response back from the system,
        we will know which response is for which request.

      · OperationKey: This is a specialized string which contains
        almost everything about the request: the catalog entry
        code, the warehouse code, the values of requested
        purchase/preorder/backorder/ quantities, the values of
        available purchase/preorder/backorder quantities. This value
    Chapter 5: Inventories                     146

      plays a very important role in how the system should react in
      case there is another request. For certain RequestType(s), you
      must have the OperationKey set in InventoryRequestItem.
    · RequestType: A flag indicates how the system should treat the
      request. There are several types of request:

1 public enum InventoryRequestType

2{

    If you look into first 4 InventoryRequestTypes (Purchase, Preorder,
    Backorder, and PurchaseOrPreorder, you can see they are for
    placing order (aka they are the "first requests"). The next three
    ones are for subsequent requests, when you make some changes
    to previous requests, such as commit it (Complete), or cancel it
    (Cancel), or make it into two shipments (Split). The last one is
    reversed for custom implementation, it is not used by default.

      · Context: same as InventoryRequest, it's for the custom imple-
        mentation.

    So how do the requests work?

    When a customer places an order, the workflow
    (specifically AdjustInventoryActivity activity) will create
    InventoryRequestItems for each lineitem in the cart:
   Chapter 5: Inventories                               148

1 private InventoryRequestItem CreateRequestItem(int lineIt\

2 emIndex, InventoryRequestType requestType,

3                                                string en\

4 tryCode, string warehouseCode, decimal delta)

5{

6   return new InventoryRequestItem(index, requestType, e\

7 ntryCode, warehouseCode, delta, string.Empty, null);

8}

   requestType  can        be  InventoryRequestType.Preorder,

   InventoryRequestType.Purchase or InventoryRequestType.Backorder,

   depends on the type of order. Note that it does not have to be

   strictly one "request item" per line item. By default, the activity

   might create two request items for one item if it must be fulfilled

   with both order and backorder. Now we have request item(s) for

   each LineItem, time to add up an InventoryRequest and make the

   request to the system:

1 var request = new InventoryRequest(DateTime.UtcNow, reque\
2 stItems, null);
3 var response = _inventoryService.Request(request);

    With the old system, when you're done with adjusting the inven-
    tory, you are ... done. No other operations needed. However, the
    new system requires extra processing - it processes your request
    and returns something. You'll need to act on that `response' some-
    how - so the next time you want to make a change on the request
    you made earlier. Let's see what in that InventoryResponse:
    Chapter 5: Inventories                         149

1 public class InventoryResponse

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets or sets a value indicating if the overall op\

5 eration was a success.

    So we only interest with what're in InventoryResponseItem. It's a
    rather big class, which you can find its definition here¹¹. However,
    we only need to care about a special property - OperationKey. It
    contains almost every thing for later use.

    What is an OperationKey? Here's an example of an operation key:

    ZZZSZPAU7GKDBPAPWK29X4KW8WQQ7VS7MPJ99Y6SWJT9XBKW6W99SVAM

        ¹¹http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/

    D5082996
    Chapter 5: Inventories                 150

    It might look cryptic to you (and you don't really have to care
    about its value), but it's actually simple than you would think - It's
    just a serialized string contains information about a request, and is
    handled by OperationKeySerializer:

1 public class OperationKeySerializer

2{

3   public virtual string Serialize(InventoryRequestType \

4 requestType, InventoryChange change);

5   public virtual bool TryDeserialize(string value, out \

6 InventoryRequestType requestType,

7            out InventoryChange change);

8}

    So an OperationKey contains information about an
    InventoryRequestType, and an InventoryChange, and it can
    be converted back and forth from those values. InventoryChange
    is a rather simple class, contains the code of the entry, the code of
    the warehouse and the "changes" of inventory values:

1 public class InventoryChange : InventoryKey, IReadOnly<In\

2 ventoryChange>

3{

4   public virtual string CatalogEntryCode { get; set; } \

5 //from InventoryKey

6   public virtual string WarehouseCode { get; set; } //f\

7 rom InventoryKey

8   public virtual decimal PurchaseAvailableChange { get;\

9 set; }

10  public virtual decimal PreorderAvailableChange { get;\

11 set; }

12  public virtual decimal BackorderAvailableChange { get\

13 ; set; }

14  public virtual decimal PurchaseRequestedChange { get;\

15 set; }
    Chapter 5: Inventories  151

16    public virtual decimal PreorderRequestedChange { get;\

17 set; }

18    public virtual decimal BackorderRequestedChange { get\

19 ; set; }

20 }

    So OperationKey is a changeset for a specific request. When the
    InventoryResponse is returned to the request, the order system
    needs to store the information - OperationKeys somewhere. Nat-
    urally, it belongs to the Shipment. Yes Shipment has a "secret" field
    OperationKeys, and here's it looks like:

    0:1#ZZZSZPAU7GKDBPAPWK29X4KW8WQQ7VS7MPJ99Y6SWJT9XBKW6W99SV
    1:1#ZZZS7PAU7GKDB2VQMPTQ8KYD7M92XEYNWG9964NLLPGXEEYAWM926R

    This value can be accessed by Shipment.OperationsKeyMap
    property. OperationsKeyMap is of type ConcurrentDictionary<int,
    IEnumerable<string>>, so for each line item index, we have a
    collection of OperationKey attached to it. Normally, we have
    only one OperationKey per line item, - but there are cases, like
    when you send a split request, which returns two OperationKey.
    Those OperationKeys are required for later processing, such as
    completing or cancelling a shipment.

    And then it comes to the time when we actually ship the or-
    der, or in the bad case, when the customer cancels the order.
    We just need to send requests with the saved OperationKey and
    InventoryRequestType - depends on the type of action we want to
    do, either Complete or Cancel.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                 152

1 private void SendRequests(Shipment shipment, InventoryReq\

2 uestType requestType)

3{

4     var itemIndexStart = 0;

5     var requestItems = shipment.OperationKeysMap.SelectMa\

6 ny(c => c.Value).Select(key =>

7        new InventoryRequestItem()

8        {

9                           ItemIndex = itemIndexStart++,

10                          OperationKey = key,

11                          RequestType = requestType

12       }).ToList();

13

14    if (requestItems.Any())

15    {

16       InventoryService.Request(new InventoryRequest(Dat\

17 eTime.UtcNow, requestItems, null));

18       shipment.ClearOperationKeys();

19    }

20 }

                  Note that after sending the request, we need
                  to call ClearOperationKeys. The data stored in
                  OperationKeys is no longer needed in the future, and
                  we should prevent any wrongdoing with that data.

    Normally, we changes all the items in batch for each shipment. But
    you can of course change an item individually, for example if the
    customer no longer wants to remove specific item from the order.
    In such cases, you can simply send a Cancel request for that item,
    and then remove it from the OperationKeyMaps.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                     153

It's a bit trickier if the customer does not want to can-
cel the item, but to change quantity. The "standard"
way to it is to cancel the original line item and request
a new one with new quantity.

So what actually happens under the hood?

When a request is made to the inventory system, depends
on the type of the request, let's say in the most common
case, Purchase, the values stored in InventoryService table
are updated. PurchaseAvailableQuantity is decreased, while
PurchaseRequestedQuantity is increased. (In cases multiple
requests are made, for example, both Purchase and BackOrder,
the respected values will be all updated). There is a sustainable
different compared to the old inventory system here. In the old
system, the values are set by the computed values from application
layer. As we mentioned earlier, this will cause issues in concurrent
cases where two or more threads want to set the values at the
same time. In the new system, the values are "adjusted" - the
API:s only let the database know which changes are needed, and
the database will handle concurrent itself. I would doubt that we
can write better, more robust concurrent handling code than SQL
Server engine itself.

When a shipment is shipped, the Complete request will make sure
the change is permanent - the value in PurchaseRequestedQuantity
will be decreased. However if the shipment is cancelled before it is
shipped, the change will be reverted back -
Chapter 5: Inventories                                   154

When should you cancel a request? It only makes
senses to cancel when the shipment has not been
shipped, or even the goods have not been leaving
the shelf. In case the goods have been packaged, or
shipped, you will need to handle it separately - to
treat the goods as outlet, for example. That's the
reason when we ship the goods, we use the Complete
request: once the shipment is shipped, the inventory
system has done its parts. It entirely depends on your
business rules on how would you treat the shipped-
but-cancelled goods.

I think the new inventory system is pretty well de-
signed (bar the APIs which could have been better).
However, it's still not 100% bullet proof. If you have
some items selling like hotcake, and if your website is
running on multiple servers/instances, it could result
in the overstock booking. There could be in theory
more safeguard regarding that, but it could be too
heavy to handle. Moral of theory: you could have a
high performance system, or you could have a strictly
correct one, but a high performance, strictly correct
one is extremely difficult to get right.

            Extending inventory system

A question I get from time to time is that if we can extend the
inventory system to contain more information. Just like warehouse
and price, the answer is, unfortunately, no. Unlike the metadata
system, you cannot just add a field into InventoryRecord and let
the framework handles it for you. If you want to add additional
information to InventoryRecord, these are the steps:
Chapter 5: Inventories  155

    · Define your own class that inherits from InventoryRecord
      with additional properties.

    · Create your own table for inventory information, usually, just
      copy from InventoryService and add the columns that match
      those properties.

    · Create your own stored procedures to manipulate data from
      that table.

    · Implement your own IInventoryService with those SP(s).
      You would also need to consider adding caching for those
      methods.

So as you can see, it's doable, but definitely not easy nor trivial
task. It is a significant investment that would make you think
twice before doing it. Maybe, a better solution is to store those
information else where.

This is also applied to price system (and it's even more complicated
as we have two price systems, and IPriceService default imple-
mentation stores/reads data from three tables).
   Part 3: The order
   system - selling is
what (really) matters

No matter how shiny your website is, no matter how cool your URL
is, and no matter how fancy the technologies you used to build it
- in the end, it's the orders you take that really matter - the most
important measure to a success of a e-Commerce website.
This part will be focus on how the order system works, and how
you can do better.
   Chapter 6: The order
             system

In the previous version of this book (Pro Episerver Commerce), this
chapter was about the underlying, concrete default implementa-
tions of the order system. Time has passed and things have changes.
Now if you write any new Optimizely Commerce Cloud code, you
should be using the interfaces. This chapter will be rewritten to
make sure those are the focus. Still fancy how things work under
the hood? Next chapter then!

             Why a new system

If you worked with older versions of Episerver Commerce, you
most likely used the old system. A question is raised naturally -
why a new system. The order system is "not so bad".

The answer is - it is not really good, neither. And here are some-
things it is not great at:

    · Performance: The design of PurchaseOrder and ShoppingCart
      as two metaclasses of OrderGroup, so they're entitled to share
      the same base table (OrderGroup) in database: whenever you
      place an order, it's actually a cart being deleted and a purchase
      order being created. Two operations in same table, you know
      it's bad for performance, especially there is an index there. It's
      even worse with the bad practice of saving the cart whenever
      a customer adds or removes an item from the cart. Imagine
      you have hundreds or even thousands of customers browsing
      your website, it'll kill the performance of placing orders.
Chapter 6: The order system  158

    · The API:s lack of abstraction. You can't just save the orders to
      somewhere else. (You can, of course, take the order and save
      it in another place, but it does mean that you are saving to
      two places, and end up loading from two places). And lacking
      of abstraction also mean it's hard to unit test. It's almost
      impossible to mock those classes. This is a very frequent
      complain about CartHelper - although it's not in the core
      API:s. It's like a wrapper for Cart which is, again, hard (if
      not possible) to mock.

    · The quirks of Lineitem-Shipment relation

The new order system is promised to solve those problems. In a very
simplified way to explain, a new abstraction layer is added, and the
old system is the default underlying implementation. Let's see how
those promises are fulfilled.

          Serializable cart mode

Serializable cart mode was introduced in Commerce 10.2 as a way
to achieve high performance. You might ask - how serializable cart
helps?

Serializable cart - as the name implies, is serialized into a big
JSON, and saved at once. Contrast to previous design (cart is just a
different order group, and sharing many same tables), this allows
the cart to be saved by one row of one table.

This approach has several advantages compared to the old one: -
Order system is freed from competition for queries/updates/deletes,
so it can be performed better, especially during high load - You are
saving to one table, instead of multiple tables at once, so that would
improve performance, on average.

Switching to serializable cart mode is voluntary, and if you do that,
there is a job to migrate existing carts into serializable carts. It's one
Chapter 6: The order system  159

way street though, you can, technically revert back to the legacy
cart mode, but the carts will not be migrated (not unless you do it
yourself).

              In retrospect, serializable cart is not as good idea as ini-
              tially thought. Yes it was a significant improvement,
              but it has several drawbacks:

    · It is impossible to search for carts with specific attributes,
      such as carts containing certain lineitems. You can technically
      use Like %keywork% query on Data column, but it's very
      ineffective and impractical to use in real life.

    · For the same reason, even a tiniest change requires saving
      of the Data column again. If you have a B2B implementation
      with big carts - i.e. a cart has a lot of small changes, this can
      be expensive. You can remedy this by only save the cart once
      per request, but it will still be a problem.

    · As the carts are deserialized/serialized, and the size of Data
      can be significant, it results in more object allocations than
      you would like. While that might not directly affect your web-
      site performance, it indirectly increases chances of garbage
      collection, even Gen 2 ones. If you have a lot of carts being
      modified at the same time, it can be a trouble.

The first two issues could be solved by leveraging the JSON han-
dling capacities in newer versions of SQL Server. However none of
that was planned.

              I did a load test with conversion rate 100%, i.e. 100%
              visitors place order. That is of course unrealistic, just
              to see how Optimizely Commerce can cope with order
              placement. I made about 300.000 orders in 50 minutes,
              with 5 minutes warm up. The limitation, turned out,
              is that the database can only cope with that much
              operations from serializable cart. Changing a big cart
              is taxing to the database, especially log.
Chapter 6: The order system                           160

Moving to serializable cart is by just running this:

1 _featureSwitch.EnableFeature(SerializedCarts.FeatureSeria\

2 lizedCarts);

    and _featureSwitch is an IFeatureSwitch.

    There will be a scheduled job that migrates carts from old system
    to serialized carts. In later version of Commerce, Serializable cart
    mode will be enabled by default for newly created sites.

                   Performance consideration - I have once asked about
                   a case when customer has very high database usage,
                   specifically, log transaction. Upon investigation, it
                   turned out that they have a very big cart, which is
                   200MB in size. As every change to that cart result
                   in a write into Data column, it will also write that
                   much data into log. You can imagine how the log
                   was almost choked. Moral of the story - if you have
                   serializable carts, make sure you don't have any big
                   carts. The threshold is debatable but I would say not
                   more than 1MB of data. You can check by this query

                       SELECT top 1000 CartId, len(DATA) AS SIZE FROM

                   SerializableCart ORDER BY SIZE DESC. Note that
                   LEN returns the length of characters, the actual size
                   in bytes is 2x of that.

        Everything is an interface.

Well, almost. In previous chapter, you learned about OrderGroup,
OrderForm, Shipment, and LineItem. Those are concrete
MetaClass(es). As they are tightly coupled to the database, it's
almost impossible to write test for them, and very difficult to have
custom implementation. To allow the required abstraction, new
    Chapter 6: The order system                       161

    interfaces were introduced: IOrderGroup, IOrderForm, IShipment,
    ILineItem, IOrderAddress. Let's take a look at those interfaces:

   You might notice IExtendedProperties - it's the core of all other
   abstractions, as it represents the concept of MetaClass for the
   abstraction. It's a rather simple interface:

1 public interface IExtendedProperties

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Gets the property bag for dealing with custom met\

5 a fields.

6   /// </summary>

7   Hashtable Properties { get; }

8}

   That's it. You can store the collection of MetaFields there.

             I personally think Hashtable is a poor choice here.
             IDictionary<string, object> could have been better,
             but it's too late for such change.

    Like IOrderGroup, IOrderForm, IShipment and other interfaces are
    implemented in an explicit way. So if you have an OrderGroup,
    which implements IOrderGroup, you might not access its Forms by
    simply call

1 orderGroup.Forms

    but

1 ((IOrderGroup)orderGroup).Forms

    You might see it as a waste, wouldn't it easier to implement
    it as the property of the implementation? However, the explicit
    interface implementation will enforce the way you work with
    API:s. You should only work with interface without having to care
    Chapter 6: The order system                          163

    about implementations. By that, when you want to change your
    implementation, such as adding new storage option, you don't have
    to change the code you wrote. It also enforce the decoupling with
    the implementation, and makes it easier for you to mock and write
    unit tests for your code.

             IOrderRepository.

    IOrderRepository is your new friend.

    In previous part, we learned about IOrderGroup. It is the base
    interface of ICart, IPurchaseOrder and IPaymentPlan. All of them
    can be managed by IOrderRepository.

    IOrderRepository is quite generic "repository" interface, nothing
    really stands out:

    You might notice the OrderReference property. It's not related
    ContentReference, but it has something in common. As its name
    might suggest, it's a identifier to an IOrder. It has four properties,
    but only OrderGroupId (which is the id) and OrderType (which is
    the type) really matter. OrderReference is the way to work with
    orders in a repository pattern - using IOrderRepository. Let's see
    how OrderReference looks like:

1 public class OrderReference

2{

3     public OrderReference(int orderGroupId, string name, \

4 Guid customerId, Type orderGroupType);

5

6     public int OrderGroupId { get; private set; }

7

8     public string Name { get; private set; }

9

10    public Guid CustomerId { get; private set; }
Chapter 6: The order system                                    166

11    public Type OrderType { get; private set; }
12
13 }

This class is, in fact, redundant. OrderGroupId and OrderType are
enough to identify an OrderGroup. Name, CustomerId can be get from
the IOrderGroup.

      With the default implementation, OrderGroupId is
      supposed be unique across the system, as all Cart,
      PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder share the same Or-
      derGroup table. However, this might change in the
      custom implementation where you save your carts
      and your purchase orders in different storages. This is
      one the main points of the new order system - allow
      you to persist the data wherever you want.

In a way, IOrderRepository can be seen to be inspired by
IContentRepository. It's one unified API:s to deal with
IOrderGroup, regardless of their true types (Cart, PurchaseOrder
or PaymentPlan). It's the providers, which know how to
work with a specific type, handle the leg work. (Remember
CatalogContentProvider?)

      If you are using Commerce 13 or earlier,

      you might notice that there are two default

      implementations        of      IOrderRepository,

      both named DefaultOrderRepository, one

      in EPiServer.Commerce.Order and one in

      EPiServer.Commerce.Order.Internal. The former

      was obsoleted and removed in Commerce 14, as

      it had some issues, and was not in an Internal

      namespace as convention. This is one of the reasons

      why you should always stick with the interfaces, not

      the concrete implementations.
Chapter 6: The order system                      167

One thing that is really missing from
IOrderRepository is the ability to handle
orders/carts/payment plans in batch. If you
really need that (and it would be very helpful
in order processing), you would have to rely on
order search API (either IOrderSearchService or
OrderContext.Search). In any case, it does not
support batch saving or batch deleting.

                   Creating orders

                         Validating carts

    Carts are not always able to convert to orders. Have you ever
    been in situations when you are going to buy something which
    is deeply discounted, or is on very limited stocks, only in the last
    step, to see price to go back to normal, or to see "Out of stock"
    message? Yes that could be frustrating as customers, but as site
    owners, it's important to not make promises you can't fulfill (i.e.
    not allowing orders to be made that won't be delivered). That is
    where OrderValidationService comes in.

    OrderValidationService is an attempt to centralize validation pro-
    cess. You might need validating the cart every time the customer
    makes changes. You might need validating it one more time before
    converting it to an order. Repeating that validation everywhere is
    probably the best idea - it's creating redundancy, and it is easier to
    make mistake. Now you can override OrderValidationService and
    custom it once. The class has one method that you can override

1 public virtual IDictionary<ILineItem, IList<ValidationIss\
2 ue>> ValidateOrder(IOrderGroup orderGroup)
Chapter 6: The order system                         168

It takes an IOrderGroup and returns a dictionary - for each
ILineItem, a list of ValidationIssue that might have caused it to
be removed, or changed in quantity. By default, ValidateOrder
does these 5 steps (simplified)

1 orderGroup.ValidateOrRemoveLineItems();
2 orderGroup.UpdatePlacedPriceOrRemoveLineItems();
3 orderGroup.UpdateInventoryOrRemoveLineItems();
4 orderGroup.ApplyDiscounts();
5 orderGroup.UpdateInventoryOrRemoveLineItems();

· It validates and removes items that are invalid (no longer
  available due end of life, or not "active" in current market
  etc.)

· It updates prices to latest available, and removes items with
  no valid prices.

· It updates quantities of items to available stocks, and removes
  items that are out of stock.

· It applies discounts

· It checks for inventories again as promotions might have
  added free items.
    Chapter 6.5: The old
        order system.

                    Overview

Order system, as same as the catalog system, is based on the
MetaDataPlus MetaClass system. That means, you can extend your
classes with additional attributes to match with your business need.
By default, it can be seen as even more complicated than the catalog
system. With Catalog system, you only have two base classes
(CatalogEntry and CatalogNode, you can consider CatalogEntryEx
and CatalogNodeEx are default classes, but they are rarely used),
but for the order system, there are quite many. Here's the list of
default order metaclasses from the default installation:

                                 Default order metaclasses

       How stuffs are structured.

On a very high level, you have order group (the order), which
contains one or more OrderForm. Each OrderForm contains one or
more Shipment, and each Shipment contains one or more LineItem.
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.                   170

    Each OrderForm contains one or more Payment, while each Shipment
    contains a ShippingAddress. Let's explore those components, one
    by one.

    At the center of the Order system, is the OrderGroup. It's base
    class of three most important classes in the system: PurchaseOrder,
    PaymentPlan and ShoppingCart D> It's a little inconsistent here, the
    class itself is Cart, but the corresponding metaclass is ShoppingCart.
    We'll use the terms interchangeably.

    Those classes share almost the same major characteristics:

        · They all have a CustomerId (which maps to a customer, ei-
          ther registered or anonymous), cost-related properties (Total,
          SubTotal, HandlingTotal etc.) and a Billing Address.

        · They all have OrderForm(s). In most of the cases, there
          will be only one OrderForm, but in some B2B systems, you
          might see two or more. Each OrderForm will have infor-
          mation about payments, promotions attached to that form.
          Each OrderForm might have one or more Shipments, a list of
          OrderAddress(es):

    · As the unit to be shipped, each shipment will have one
      Shipping address (It'll be the address of the store, in case it's
      an in-store pickup shipment), shipping method information,
      shipping cost and so on. Each Shipment has a list of LineItems
      belong to it.

    · Each LineItem has information about the the entry it rep-
      resents, the quantity, the price when it was added to cart
      (PlacedPrice) , the price considering quantity and discounts
      (ExtendedPrice), and then discounts for itself.

                  The lineitems do not really belong to the shipment.
                  They belong to the OrderForm, and the lineitems in a
                  shipment is only a subset mapped to that shipment.
                  This will be explained in later section.

    It's PaymentPlan which is a little different, because it has cycle
    information attached.

    However, those classes are different in the places they are used, and
    therefore, they are processed with different workflows, resulting in
    different behaviors. You can save a Cart in Commerce Manager
    without any payments attached, but you will need to have a valid
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  174

payment amount (which match the order's total) to be able to save
a PurchaseOrder.

And as OrderGroup and its derived classes are all MetaObject, you
can always call .AcceptChanges() on an instance of them, to get it
saved to the database. And what if you want to delete it? .Delete()
followed by .AcceptChanges() will do the job.

Let's explore those classes one by one.

                              Cart

Cart is the first step of the journey from visitor to customer. By
default convention, before a visitor can make a transaction (i.e.
place an order), he or she must create a cart first.

A cart is supposed to be unique per market, name and customer. If
the customer already has another cart in same market, same name
in the system, then when he or she try to save another cart, it would
result in an exception. Optimizely Commerce has a check to prevent
such thing from happening - when a customer browses the site, the
existing cart will be loaded if he already has one, a new one is only
created when there is none in existent.

              Of course, PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder have no
              such restriction. A customer can have as many
              PaymentPlan or PurchaseOrder in the same market as
              he/she likes.
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.            175

1 public class Cart : OrderGroup

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the default name for the cart.

5     /// </summary>

6     public static string DefaultName { get; }

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Gets the name for the Wishlist.

10    /// </summary>

11    public static string WishListName { get; }

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Gets the exchange name for the cart.

15    /// </summary>

16    public static string ExchangeName { get; }

17

18    ...

19 }

    How to create a cart? If you used very old version of Episerver
    Commerce, you will probably remember CartHelper

1 var cartHelper = new CartHelper();

    CartHelper was a helper class, provides convenient way
    to access and manipulate Cart. It's open sourced in
    Mediachase.Commerce.Website package. For a very long time, it's
    the way to create and update the shopping cart. CartHelper has
    another constructors which take the name of the cart, the customer
    Id and the market he is in. The parameter-less constructor above
    will use all the default value - the default cart name (Can you
    guess it, it's "Default"), the current customer Id (even anonymous
    customer has one) and the current market:
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.                        176

1 var cartHelper = new CartHelper(name, userId, market);

                   What is so bad about CartHelper? Mostly, because
                   it had become, over the time, the GOD class which
                   does everything about the Cart, new functions had
                   been added without much thinking about architecting
                   the class - it is doing too much. CartHelper is almost
                   impossible to mock, so it's painful to write unit tests
                   for cart-related functionalities.

    And then, we can access the Cart by:

1 var cart = cartHelper.Cart;

    When a customer adds an item to cart - you had to call a "workflow"
    - CartValidate. This workflow will check if the items in cart can be
    fulfilled (if this is a normal purchase order, if this is pre-order, or
    backorder, then the inventories must have special settings to allow
    that) and if the cart is eligible for any promotions. If an item does
    not satisfy the check, it will be removed from the cart. If there is
    a promotion which can be applied, discounts will be made to the
    price and/or gift items will be added to cart.
    There are several ways to call the workflow. The most common way
    in this stage is probably:

1 cartHelper.RunWorkflow("CartValidate");

    the other way is to use OrderGroupWorkflowManager:

1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(cartHelper.Cart, Or\
2 derGroupWorkflowManager.CartValidateWorkflowName);
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.                     177

Workflows, or ActivityFlows, have been an essential
part of Episerver Commerce since the first version.
However, with the raise of new promotion system, it's
not a mandatory thing to run anymore. For websites
which sell downloadable contents and use the new
promotion system, there is almost no reason to use
workflows.

                        The cart implementation in QuickSilver

Another special form of Cart is wishlist - it is just a Cart with
defined name by Cart.WishListName constant ("WishList", if you
wonder). Again, you can choose a different name for your wishlist,
just make sure to stick with it.
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  178

The wishlist implementation on QuickSilver, note that it displays no quantity

There are several known "best practices" for wishlist, such as you
should not care about the quantity of items in a wishlist, or you
should not make a check for inventories when an item was added
to it, or only registered, logged in users have wishlists, when a
customer adds an item from wishlist to cart, it should be removed
from wishlist. However, these are not definitive rules, and you can
of course implements your wishlist as your business requires.

              By default, when a customer logs in, his current carts
              will be merged with his existing carts in the system
              (assuming both carts have line item(s)). We will look
              into how to change that behavior later.

                       PaymentPlan

PaymentPlan is a special form of OrderGroup where you can create
and manage the cycle of recurring order. For simplest example,
think about a subscription. Your customer wants to order a mag-
azine to be delivered to his home the 5th of every month, for
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.                  179

    six months. Instead of having manually create an order for him
    every month (trust me, it's a tedious work, even if you have
    only few customers), you can create a PaymentPlan to handle such
    requirements.

                  The future is here, and it's "everything is a subscrip-
                  tion".

1 public class PaymentPlan : OrderGroup

2{

3       public virtual PaymentPlanCycle CycleMode { get; set;}

4       public virtual int CycleLength { get; set;}

5       public virtual int MaxCyclesCount { get; set;}

6       public virtual int CompletedCyclesCount { get; set;}

7       public virtual DateTime StartDate { get; set;}

8       public virtual DateTime EndDate { get; set;}

9       public virtual DateTime LastTransactionDate { get; se\

10 t;}

11      public virtual bool IsActive { get; set;}

12      ...

13 }

    PaymentPlan is really flexible in term of cycle, it allows you to set
    whatever cycle your customers would like:
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.             180

1 public enum PaymentPlanCycle

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Unknown Plan Cycle

5     /// </summary>

6     None = 0,

7     /// <summary>

8     /// Daily Cycle

9     /// </summary>

10    Days = 1,

11    /// <summary>

12    /// Weekly Cycle

13    /// </summary>

14    Weeks,

15    /// <summary>

16    /// Monthly Cycle

17    /// </summary>

18    Months,

19    /// <summary>

20    /// Yearly Cycle

21    /// </summary>

22    Years,

23    /// <summary>

24    /// Custom1 Cycle, use your own time frame.

25    /// </summary>

26    Custom1,

27    /// <summary>

28    /// Custom2 Cycle, use your own time frame.

29    /// </summary>

30    Custom2

31 }
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  181

            Cycle settings for payment plan, it is simpler than it seems

              If CycleMode is None then the PaymentPlan will
              only happen once. It will behave like one-time
              PurchaseOrder.

Let's say you want an order to be created every 3 days, or every
2 weeks - that's when CycleLength comes to help - you can
set CycleMode to Days and CycleLength to 3, or CycleMode to
Weeks and CycleLength to 2, respectively. Combine CycleMode and
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  182

CycleLength and your payment plans can fit into even most com-
plicated subscriptions. You can control the number of subscription
to be made, by setting the value of StartDate and EndDate, and
MaxCyclesCount.

              StartDate and EndDate can be used to set the limit
              of the cycle if you know the time it would end
              (when calculating the number of cycle is not triv-
              ial, like having an order every week, for 6 months),
              while MaxCyclesCount can be used when you know
              exactly the number of times you should convert the
              PaymentPlan to purchase order (having 30 orders, ev-
              ery other week). Those are not exclusive, they can be
              used in conjunction and which condition is reached
              first will be used.

    You will usually not create a PaymentPlan by code directly, but to
    create a Cart first, and then convert it to a PaymentPlan:

1 var paymentPlan = cart.SaveAsPaymentPlan();
2 paymentPlan.AcceptChanges();

    To turn a PaymentPlan to a PurchaseOrder, it's as simple as call:

1 var purchaseOrder = paymentPlan.SaveAsPurchaseOrder();

    However, you normally don't have to do it - the point of
    PaymentPlan is to make sure it can be automated once it's
    created. Episerver Commerce comes with a scheduled job,
    named "Subscription Payment Plans", which will convert any
    PaymentPlans which statisfies the conditions (.IsDue returns true)
    to PurchaseOrder. You can set this scheduled job to run daily, and
    let it does it job.
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.             183

                         PurchaseOrder

    PurchaseOrder is what you want an OrderGroup to be - it's the cart
    which has been converted successfully to an order. PurchaseOrder
    extends OrderGroup with some notable properties:

1 public class PurchaseOrder : OrderGroup

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets or sets the parent order group id. This can \

5 be used to relate purchase orders to a payment plan or ot\

6 her order.

7     /// </summary>

8     public int ParentOrderGroupId { get; set;}

9

10    /// <summary>

11    /// Tracking number. Automatically generated or can b\

12 e generated using provided function.

13    /// </summary>

14    public string TrackingNumber { get; set;}

15

16    /// <summary>

17    /// Expiration date can be used for subscription type\

18 of orders.

19    /// </summary>

20    public DateTime ExpirationDate { get; set;}

22           /// <summary>

23    /// The collection of Return merchandise authorizatio\

24 n (RMA).

25           /// </summary>

26    public OrderFormCollection ReturnOrderForms { get; }

27 }

    There are several ways to create an order, but normally you will
    Chapter 6.5: The old order system.                184

    call SaveAsPurchaseOrder method on a Cart or PaymentPlan. For
    example, this is the normal way to create a PurchaseOrder:

1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(Cart, OrderGroupWor\

2 kflowManager.CartCheckOutWorkflowName, true, dic);

3 Cart.CustomerId = PrincipalInfo.CurrentPrincipal.GetConta\

4 ctId();

5 var po = Cart.SaveAsPurchaseOrder();

6

7 if (_currentContact != null)

8{

9     Cart.CustomerName = _currentContact.FullName;

10 }

11

12 OrderNotesManager.AddNoteToPurchaseOrder(purchaseOrder, n\

13 oteDetail, OrderNoteTypes.System, PrincipalInfo.CurrentPr\

14 incipal.GetContactId());

15 po.AcceptChanges();

16

17 // Remove old cart

18 Cart.Delete();

19 Cart.AcceptChanges();

    Note that this code has been simplified. But the idea is simple: You
    run the CartCheckout workflow, as it will process payments, adjust
    inventories, and calculate the totals of the order. Then you save the
    Cart as a PurchaseOrder, add some more information to it (in this
    case, adding an OrderNote), then save it again. Finally, you delete
    the old Cart.

    It's worth noting that .SaveAsPurchaseOrder (as well as
    .SaveAsPaymentPlan) does not really "transform" the Cart
    into a PurchaseOrder, instead, it creates a new PurchaseOrder
    and copy the data over. Now we have both the Cart and the
    PurchaseOrder existing in the system. That's why we need to call
    the last two lines - the old Cart should be deleted.
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  185

        OrderForm - Shipment - LineItem

        Payments and Shipping Methods

      The quirk of Shipment and LineItem
                        relationship

If you ask me one thing that I hate the most about Episerver
Commerce, I can answer without hesitation: the index-quantity
mapping between Shipment and LineItem.

Why?

At its core, it's the OrderForm which owns the collection of LineIt-
ems. In case you have only one shipment, then the LineItems
collection belong to that shipment entirely, all is good. Let's see
when you have more than one shipment, which is a quite common
scenario, especially when you have a B2B solution.

The collection of LineItems is still there, and the OrderForm still
owns it. However, to distribute the lineitems across the shipments,
there is the concept of index-quantity mapping. So let's say we
have lineitem A, B, C with the quantity of 3,4,2, respectively. The
distribution in the first shipment is A:1, B:2, C:1, and in the second
shipment is A:2, B:2, C:1.

To store that information, the indices of the lineitems was used, so
they will be 0:1, 1:2, 2:1 and 0:2, 1:2, 2:1, respectively. All is still good,
at this point.

The problem arises when you want to remove one lineitem, change
it quantity or move it around (to another shipment, for example).
That's when the index-quantity max shows it true face: fragile.
You can easily get the wrong quantity, or even worse, get an
IndexOutOfRangeException just because a lineitem was removed
but the map was not updated.

It's something in design and you just can't avoid it entirely. But
Chapter 6.5: The old order system.  186

you can still avoid those problems by using methods provided
in PurchaseOrderManager, instead of manipulating the LineItems
collection your own.

              Trust me, a good amount of power brain has been
              spent to make sure those methods work correctly and
              reliably.
     Part 4: Events -
   Listen and react

One of the key features of Episerver Commerce is the ability to
integrate into external systems. To do that, parts of Commerce
need to communicate, to let other parts know that they are doing
something/something have been done. The most common way of
doing so is to use events. Thankfully, Episerver Framework already
provides a strong mechanism for firing and catching events, so
Commerce only has to care about when to raise events and what
to raise from it.
       Chapter 8: Content
               events.

    As catalog content is also "CMS" content, you can use
    IContentEvents if you are using content API:s exclusively.
    As CatalogContentProvider is just a provider in the content
    system, the events are automatically fired from the pipeline
    whenever you make changes to the catalog content. To make it
    easier to understand, let's take a simple example. Let's assume you
    want to check every time a content is being moved via Catalog
    UI, you want to do some extra checks, if not, you will cancel the
    operation, you can have something like this:

1 using EPiServer;

2 using EPiServer.Commerce.Catalog.ContentTypes;

3 using EPiServer.Core;

4 using System;

5

6 public class CatalogContentEventProcessor : IDisposable

7{

10 entEvents)

12     _contentEvents = contentEvents;

13     _contentEvents.MovingContent += Content_Moving;

14  }

15

17 ntArgs e)

Chapter 8: Content events.                                                              189

23          }

27    {
28

30 }        _contentEvents.MovingContent -= Content_Moving;
      }

There are plenty of events from IContentEvents that you can listen-
ing from, with the full list here¹³. However, it's a good practice to
always check to see ContentEventArgs.Content is indeed a content
type you want to process.

As we learned from previous chapters, there are more to the catalog
system than just content. There are underlying layers that are not
"visible" to the content system. We will dig into those in later
sections of this chapter.

              Deleting and Deleted. Like common event nam-
              ing convention, the content events comes with pair
              of events - such as Updating/Updated, or Delet-
              ing/Deleted. By convention, the Deleting event is
              supposed to fire before it happens, while the Deleted
              is fired after. Just keep in mind that at the point of
              Deleted event, you'll no longer be able to get back the
              content. So if you want to do something with catalog
              content being deleted, make sure to use Deleting
              event.

    ¹³https://world.optimizely.com/csclasslibraries/cms/EPiServer.Core.IContentEvents?

version=11
    Chapter 8: Content events.                             190

       There is no "Waste Basket" concept in
       CatalogContentProvider. So when you delete a
       catalog content, you actually delete it, it is not
       moving to Waste Basket as normal CMS contents.

    It's convenient to use IContentEvents, but there is a
    limitation. Those events only get fired if you works on
    CatalogContentProvider level (via IContentRepository). If
    you work with lower levels, such as working with ICatalogSystem
    directly without involve the content system, those events will not
    be fired. In a perfect world, you should only have to care about
    IContentEvents, but we are still far from it. While you are no longer
    required to work with ICatalogSystem anymore, ServiceAPI is still
    using it internally¹. ServiceAPI is increasingly popular, and most
    sites I've seen are using it, or planned to use it. You'll need another
    way to get it works, that's when CatalogEventListenerBase comes
    into place, and here's what it looks like:

    How does it work? At the time your site starts up, EventContext
    in Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog will look for all registered in-
    stances of CatalogEventListenerBase, and then register their meth-
    ods to its events. When certain is made to catalog system, for
    example when a CatalogEntryDto is saved, a relevant method of
    EventContext will be called, and it will fire the event itself, and the
    methods registered with that event will be triggered.

    Simple, right?

    Now let's consider two cases of processing events with
    CatalogEventListenerBase:

    The first one is when you listen to the events from the same context
    (aka local event). For example, you have ServiceAPI running in the
    context of their site, and every time your Windows service import
Chapter 8: Content events.  192

something via ServiceAPI to your system, you want to listen to
them and do some special indexing.

The good news is this is the simple case. You can just create your
own implementation of CatalogEventListenerBase and override the
method you want to listen, then register your class like this:

              Note that you don't have to override all the methods,
              just the ones you want to listen to. Build it, and it
              should be ready to go.

The second one is a more advanced scenario, you want to have
ServiceAPi to run in a separate site, may be because of performance
issues or security concerns. But when you import entries and nodes
into that ServiceAPI instance, you want your site to listen to those
events and act accordingly, probably to do some extra processing
(clearing cache, indexing, etc. ). You want it to be "just ServiceAPI",
so it means simpler to configure and maintain.

In other words, you need to listen to remote events and act.

In such case, CatalogEventListenerBase can't help you, at least
directly. It is supposed to listen to local events, and it has to be
in same context when you fire the events. You don't have both.

But luckily, the solution comes from one existing implementation
of CatalogEventListenerBase - CatalogEventBroadcaster. As its
name might suggest, this class is used to broadcast events, even
remotely.

              The following section will be an exercise to work with

                 CatalogEventBroadcaster

CatalogEventBroadcaster uses Episerver Framework Events inter-
nally, so it might be worth to learn one thing or two about Events.
If you are using Azure or DXP as most of the customers, events
   Chapter 8: Content events.           193

   will be sent via Azure ServiceBus. Otherwise on on-premise setup,
   events will be broadcasted via UDP, with two important data: a
   GUID to identify the event (So listeners on listen to the events they
   care about), and a byte array contains the information from the
   sender.

   Now get back to the problem we have. CatalogEventBroadcaster
   use Events to raise the event remotely, so we need to know its event
   "Id", which is CatalogEventBroadcaster.CommerceProductUpdated

   The first part is registering a method to listen to this event in your
   class. Note that this class can be an ordinary class - it does not have
   to inherit from CatalogEventListenerBase:

1 public void AddEvent()

2{

3     var catalogEventUpdatedEvent = Event.Get(CatalogEvent\

4 Broadcaster.CommerceProductUpdated);

5     catalogEventUpdatedEvent.Raised += CatalogEventUpdate\

6 d;

7}

   You'll need to call this method during your site initialization
   (Strictly speaking, you don't have to, you can register when you
   really want to process the event. But in most of the cases, you
   should register when your site starts.), there are several ways to
   do that, but's here the pattern I usually follow:
   Chapter 8: Content events.                             194

1 //Inside an IInitializationModule

2 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

3{

4     var listener = context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<Ca\

5 talogContentEventListener>();

6     listener.AddEvent();

7}

   It's also important to deregister your method from the event to
   avoid possible memory leaks, so let's create another method to do
   that:

1 public void RemoveEvent()

2{

3     var catalogEventUpdatedEvent = Event.Get(CatalogEvent\

4 Broadcaster.CommerceProductUpdated);

5     catalogEventUpdatedEvent.Raised -= CatalogEventUpdate\

6 d;

7}

   And this method should be called in Uninitialize:

1 //Inside your IInitializationModule

2 public void Uninitialize(InitializationEngine context)

3{

4     _listener.RemoveEvent();

5}

   The remaining work is to implement CatalogEventUpdated method,
   which should have a signature like this:

1 private void CatalogEventUpdated(object sender, EventNoti\
2 ficationEventArgs e)
   Chapter 8: Content events.                          195

   The information carried with the CatalogEventBroadcaster events
   (stored in EventNotificationEventArgs.Param) are byte[] contain
   serialized data, so let's deserialize it back to object:

1 private static EventArgs DeSerialize(byte[] buffer)

2{

3   var formatter = new BinaryFormatter();

4   using (var stream = new MemoryStream(buffer))

5   {

6      return formatter.Deserialize(stream) as EventArgs;

7   }

8}

   Now depends on the events you want to listen, you'll
   have to cast EventArgs returned by DeSerialize to
   CatalogContentUpdateEventArgs, and check for its EventType
   property, which is a string.

   These are the events you can listen to:

You might argue that the BinaryFormatter is slow
to use for Serialization and Deserialization, and
you might want to use something faster - but
that does mean you'll need to implement your
own CatalogEventBroadcaster. In many cases
BinaryFormatter is fast enough and you can get
better results by optimizing something else.
  Chapter 9: Inventories,
Prices and Orders events

     Inventories and prices events

              While inventories are changed frequently and are less
              likely to get benefit from these changes, price events
              can be really useful - you should index an entry when
              its prices are changed.

Inventories and Prices events share many common characteristics.
They are both based on CatalogKeyEventBroadcaster. The idea is
the same with CatalogEventBroadcaster - when a change is made
to inventory or price system, the implementations of those system
will fire an event. Listeners will have to listen to that event and
react.

              If you are using Find.Commerce, it's probably good
              to know that it listen to prices and inventories events
              and will automatically reindex the content. However,
              the mechanism of Find.Commerce to load content is
              not the best and can cause hit in performance when
              there are a lot of changes.

                 Orders events

There are also certain events to listen and react when an or-
der is changed - either updated or deleted. Those events are in
Chapter 9: Inventories, Prices and Orders events  199

OrderContext class and are defined like this:

 1 /// <summary>
 2 /// Occurs when an order group is updated.
 3 /// </summary>
 4 public event EventHandler<OrderGroupEventArgs> OrderGroup\
 5 Updated;
 6
 7 /// <summary>
 8 /// Occurs when an order group is deleted.
 9 /// </summary>
10 public event EventHandler<OrderGroupEventArgs> OrderGroup\
11 Deleted;

     So if you want to do some extra processing when an order is
     updated, you can register your listener in an initialization module:

1 //Register to the event:
2 OrderContext.OrderGroupUpdated += OrderGroupUpdated;
3
4 //Listen to the event
5 private void OrderGroupUpdated(object sender, OrderGroupE\
6 ventArgs e)

    Those events are for OrderGroup - when an OrderGroup is changed
    - via AcceptChanges(), the corresponding event will be fired. If you
    recall from previous chapter, an OrderGroup can be a Purchase-
    Order, a PaymentPlan, or a Cart. So if you want to listen to changes
    made to PurchaseOrder:s only, you will need a little casting:
    Chapter 9: Inventories, Prices and Orders events   200

1 //Listen to the event

2 private void OrderGroupUpdated(object sender, OrderGroupE\

3 ventArgs e)

4{

5     var po = sender as PurchaseOrder;

6     if (po != null)

7     {

8              // do your stuffs here.

9     }

10 }

    It's worth noting that the orders events, unlike the events we talked
    about, are local-only. So if you have multiple sites and an order is
    changed in one site, then the listeners will not be called in other
    sites. However, I don't think there is a need for you to fire events
    in one site and process in another one.

    If you are using IOrderReposity (And as usual, you should), you
    should also be using IOrderEvents. Yes it is inspired heavily by
    IContentEvents, but IOrderEvents is a rather simple interface with
    only 4 methods:

1 public interface IOrderEvents

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Is called before an order is saved.

5     /// </summary>

6     event EventHandler<OrderEventArgs> SavingOrder;

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Is called after an order is saved.

10    /// </summary>

11    event EventHandler<OrderEventArgs> SavedOrder;

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Is called before an order is deleted.
    Chapter 9: Inventories, Prices and Orders events             201

15    /// </summary>
16    event EventHandler<OrderEventArgs> DeletingOrder;
17
18    /// <summary>
19    /// Is called after an order is deleted.
20    /// </summary>
21    event EventHandler<OrderEventArgs> DeletedOrder;
22 }

    As you might have guessed, the V-ingOrder events are before the
    data is committed, and the V-edOrder ones are after. If you, for
    example, want to look for data of a deleted order, it might be a
    good idea to do that in DeletingOrder event.

         It's worth noting that IOrderEvents is marked as
         BETA, meaning it could be changed without a major
         version. The intention was not the methods them-
         selves would be changed, but it is to allow new events
         to be added. So far, that hasn't happened, but who
         knows.

    This is a simple example of how to use IOrderEvents

1 public class OrderEventListener

2{

3     private readonly IOrderRepository _orderRepository;

4     private readonly IOrderEvents _orderEvents;

5     private readonly ILogger _logger = LogManager.GetLogg\

6 er(typeof(OrderEventListener));

9 itory, IOrderEvents orderEvents)

10    {

11       _orderRepository = orderRepository;

12       _orderEvents = orderEvents;

13     }
