15     public void AddEvents()

16     {

17        _orderEvents.SavedOrder += OrderEventsOnSavedOrde\

18 r;

19        _orderEvents.DeletingOrder += OrderEventsOnDeleti\

20 ngOrder;

21     }

22

23     private void OrderEventsOnSavedOrder(object sender, O\

24 rderEventArgs orderEventArgs)

25     {

26        var po = orderEventArgs.OrderGroup as IPurchaseOr\

27 der;

28        if (po != null)

29        {

30           _logger.Information($"Order {po.OrderNumber} \

31 was saved");

32        }

33     }

34

35     private void OrderEventsOnDeletingOrder(object sender\

36 , OrderEventArgs orderEventArgs)

37     {

38        var cart = _orderRepository.Load<ICart>(orderEven\

39 tArgs.OrderLink.OrderGroupId);

40        if (cart != null)

41        {

42           _logger.Information($"Cart '{cart.Name}' for \

43 user '{cart.CustomerId}' is being deleted.");

44        }

45     }

46

47     public void RemoveEvents()
    Chapter 9: Inventories, Prices and Orders events               203

50 ngOrder;

54 }

    And then you can call AddEvents and RemoveEvents in one of your
    IInitializationModule.

             IOrderEvents is the future for order events, just
             like IOrderRepository. Just remember that it only
             works if you are using IOrderRepository. If you are,
             for one reason for another, using lower level APIs,
             such as PurchaseOrder itself, you would have to use
             OrderContext.
     Part 5: Search

Search has become more and more important part of any software
system. When the data is big enough, search is essential, you and
your customers don't want to, and can't, go through endless lists to
find the product you want. A good commerce site can't be lacking
a fast, effective search and filter solutions to let customers quickly
find things they need - and even better - to discover things they
might want. In this chapter, we will explore the searching options
Episerver Commerce provides, and how can you use it in your
website implementation.
    Chapter 11: Search -
  where and how to find

              stuffs

          Searching for products.

                    SearchProviders

The first option we are going to talk about is the search provider
system in Commerce. It's basically index-based search system that
many important features in Commerce Manager use index-based
search provider to search for entries - it's simply faster and more
flexible to work with. They might not be truly "real-time", but
they're close (with the right configuration). The only drawbacks
are they require extra processing (for indexing entries), external
files/configurations, but the advantages easily overweight any of
those drawbacks.

              It's also possible to find entries by SQL-based APIs -
              we will get to that later.

For search providers to work, they need "index" of the entries. An
extra, prerequisite step is required: each and every entry must be
indexed for later search. All of 1st party search providers are built
on Lucene index, which if you are interested, you can read more
about how the documents are indexed here¹

    ¹http://lucene.apache.org/core/4_10_2/core/org/apache/lucene/codecs/lucene410/package-

summary.html#package_description
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  206

There are three search providers provided by Episerver
Commerce: LuceneSearchProvider, Solr35SearchProvider, and
FindSearchProvider (four if you count SolrSearchProvider as well.
As other "provider system", you can implement your own search
provider.):

    · LuceneSearchProvider comes as the default option with EPiS-
      erver.Commerce.Core, and it's the only one does not require
      extra configurations - it can just work out of the box. Luce-
      neSearchProvider is based on Lucene.net to index and query
      "documents", and the index files will be stored locally. (Not
      strictly locally as you can put them on a shared network drive,
      but your IIS instance must have direct access to the files)

    · Solr35SearchProvider is a hosted search provider solution. So
      you need to configure a Solr server to store indexes, and
      to handle search queries through HTTP requests. General
      understanding is that Solr provides better performance and
      scalability than Lucene.net, but that comes at a cost - more
      configuration and more maintenance.

    · FindSearchProvider is comparable with Solr35SearchProvider
      as it works with an external service via HTTP requests.
      Only this time it's Episerver Find. As we will learn later,
      FindSearchProvider != Find.Commerce.

So which provider is right for you? LuceneSearchProvider works
pretty well to some points, but after that, it struggles when the
catalog becomes big enough, or when it has to process high number
of queries at the same time. When that happens, it might be
the time for you to consider more scalable search providers -
Solr35SearchProvider, FindSearchProvider, or even better, Find.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs       207

Find.Commerce is an additional layer of Find API for
Commerce (catalog contents, prices, inventories etc.).
It allows more effective indexing and searching of
catalog content, and it allows auto reindex of cata-
log content when prices and/or inventories change.
Without Find.Commerce, catalog content will still be
indexed as they are still IContent, but some necessary
conventions will be missing.

                 Where SearchProvider is used

It can be confusing to understand where the SearchProvider is used
or how does it distinct from EPiServer Search? It can be easier to
grasp the idea if you remember that SearchProvider concept came
from a Commerce background. Despite of a big effort Episerver
have tried to integrate Commerce into CMS, they are still two
sub-systems working together at this time of writing (and will be,
at least in near future). They are both index-based search system,
but with a very important difference. EPiServer Search works with
CMS pages, while SearchProvider works only with catalog entries.

If you are going to implement your own search provider, you
would need to know where your provider is gonna be used. Search
Provider is used heavily in Commerce Manager - As mentioned
above, Commerce Manager uses SearchProvider in almost search-
related functionalities, either when you search to edit an entry, or
when you add a lineitem to an order, or when you set an entry to
be a gift in a promotion.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  208

                 New Line Item dialog - powered by SearchProvider

There is still one exception: Search by code or id in Catalog
Management use SQL search internally.

    Search By Code/Id is the only place which does not use SearchProvider

In Editing UI, Catalog UI uses SearchProvider internally in the
quick search box, but returns all entries including inactive (which
is different from default searches which hide the inactive entries)
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  209

                              Search box use SearchProvider

And Global search uses SearchProvider as well, search results from
SearchProvider are combined with results from other searches:
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs        210

                         Global search

What happens if you use Find? SearchProvider will
works (if properly configured) - they are not mutu-
ally exclusive, even if you use FindSearchProvider.
FindSearchProvider uses Find internally for storing
and querying the indices, but in "its own way", not the
"strongly typed contents" as FindCommerce does. The
indices for Find and for FindSearchProvider will still
be separated, even if you share the same "serviceIn-
dex" from Episerver. That would mean if you use both
Find (for front-end site) and FindSearchProvider for
Commerce Manager, you index count will be double
in size.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  211

                               Use Search API:s

    Now enough of theory. Let's go into some technical detail.
    The search provider system is build around SearchManager and
    CatalogEntrySearchCriteria. In its simplest form, searching using
    search API:s can be easy like this:

1 CatalogEntrySearchCriteria criteria = new CatalogEntrySea\
2 rchCriteria();
3 criteria.SearchPhrase = "OLED";
4 SearchManager manager = new SearchManager(AppContext.Curr\
5 ent.ApplicationName);
6 ISearchResults results = manager.Search(criteria);
7 var ids = results.GetKeyFieldValues<int>()

    We're searching for the entries with the keyword "OLED", and
    getting a list of Id:s for matching entries. From this ids, you can
    get the list of contents. Still remember ReferenceConverter and
    IContentLoader/IContentRepository?

                   Note that with the code above, any entry with a
                  "OLED" keyword in one of the indexed fields will
                   match, no matter it's in the name, the code, the display
                   name or the description (as long as the fields are
                   marked as "indexable"). What if you want to search
                   for entries with "OLED" in name, specifically - we will
                   get to that soon enough.

Let's explore more complex scenarios - how much can we search
with CatalogEntrySearchCriteria - it is not a simple class, so we
will go through most important properties, one by one.

    · CatalogNames: Limits the catalogs you want to search in. If
      you have multiple catalogs, this can be used. Let's reuse
      the example above. You might have a catalog for consumer
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  212

products and a catalog for professional products - and their
names are respectively "Consumer" and "Professional", if you
want to limit the search for entries in "Consumer" catalog
only:

1 criteria.CatalogNames.Add("Consumer");

        · CatalogNodes: Limits the catalog nodes you want to search
           in. If you want to limit the search for entries only in TV and
           Bluray nodes:

1 criteria.CatalogNodes.Add("TV");
2 criteria.CatalogNodes.Add("Bluray");

    However, you can use Outlines as an alternative

        · Outlines: The "paths" you want to search in, in form of

             CatalogName\CatalogNodes

1 criteria.Outlines.Add("Consumer\TV\OLED");

        · ClassTypes: The types of entries you want to search for
           (Product, Variation, Package or Bundle). For example, to
           search for Product only:

1 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Product");

    All properties above are of type StringCollection - and they mean
    "restrictions". Leave them empty mean you don't want to filter by
    that criteria. Note that you can add multiple criteria to one property.
    For example, you can filter by both "Product" and "Variation":
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  213

1 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Product");
2 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Variation");

    Those settings are useful, but nothing is particularly interesting -
    but the next one might be

                                      Specific filters

    In our first example we searched for "OLED", so any items
    which has an indexable field which contains "OLED". What if
    you want to search for a specific field only. This can be done
    by a method in the base class of CatalogEntrySearchCriteria -
    AbstractSearchCriteria:

1 criteria.Add("OLED", "OLED");

    Add takes two parameters, one for the field name, and one for the
    value of that field you want to filter by.
    There is also an overload that take the field name and a list of
    ISearchFilterValue, which mean you can filter by multiple values.
    You can search for "OLED" and "LG" in "displayname" like this:

1 var filters = new List<ISearchFilterValue>();
2 filters.Add(new SimpleValue { key = "displayname", value \
3 = "OLED" });
4 filters.Add(new SimpleValue { key = "displayname", value \
5 = "LG" });
6 criteria.Add("displayname", filters);

                             SearchFilter / Facets

Facet or faceted search is an important feature of any eCommerce
websites - it allows your customers to quickly filter the products
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  214

based on some criteria when they browse your product listing. For
example, here a very simple facet by Quicksilver
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  215

       It might not be the best facet in the world, but it gives you the idea

There is more than 1 way to create faceted searching, but the most
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  216

    easy, flexible and powerful way, is probably relying on the search
    functionalities. That means, your product listing page is simply
    a search-result display page (pretty common pattern, you know).
    The facets in Commerce Search API:s are done by SearchFilter.
    The idea is pretty simple - you define the filters you want your
    customers to see, and for every search, the search results will
    include the number of matching products for each filter. When the
    customer selects a "facet", you'll include the filter mapped with that
    in the search to limit the scope.

    Here's how facets are done in Episerver Commerce:

        · You define filters you would like to have in
          Mediachase.Search.Filters.Config, here's an example
          of a filter for field = "size":

1 <SearchConfig version="1.0">

2   <SearchFilters>

3   <Filter field="size">

4   <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

5   <Description locale="en">Size</Description>

6   <Description locale="sv">Storlek</Description>

7   </Descriptions>

8   <Values>

9   <SimpleValue key="s" value="s">

10  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

11  <Description locale="en">S</Description>

12  </Descriptions>

13  </SimpleValue>

14  <SimpleValue key="m" value="m">

15  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

16  <Description locale="en">M</Description>

17  </Descriptions>

18  </SimpleValue>

19  <SimpleValue key="l" value="l">
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  217

20     <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

21                  <Description locale="en">L</Description>

22     </Descriptions>

23     </SimpleValue>

24     </Values>

25     </Filter>

26     </SearchFilters>

27 </SearchConfig>

    Pretty simple, right? You define a filter for the field "size" - which is
    a searchable metafield. You then define the value you want to create
    filters for, in this case, "s", "m", and "l" as three possible values for
    "size". Of course, depends on your catalog, you might add the value
    for "xxs" or "XL".

    There are three type of values you can configure for the filters:

       · The simple value as we see above.
       · The price range value:

    1 <PriceRangeValue key="100-200" currency="USD" lowerboun\

    2 d="100" upperbound="200" lowerboundincluded="false" upper\

    3 boundincluded="true">

    4  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

    5               <Description locale="en">$100-$200</Description>

    6               <Description locale="de">100-200</Description>

    7  </Descriptions>

    8 </PriceRangeValue>

         (How prices are indexed will be discussed later, when we talk
         about implementing customizing the search indexer.)

       · The range value, basically everything which is "numberic",
         but price:
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs        218

1 <RangeValue key="under-1.5" upperbound="1.5">

2 <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

3  <Description locale="en">1.5 in &amp; Under</Descri\

4 ption>

5  <Description locale="de">1.5 in &amp; Under</Descri\

6 ption>

7 </Descriptions>

8 </RangeValue>

The SearchFilters configuration, in fact, is not required -
you can define your SearchFilter(s) by code, however.
SearchFilterHelper in Mediachase.Commerce.Website will
automatically deserialize this information for you, and having
these information in a separated config file should make the
configuration much more easier, without having to recompile your
code.

              It's worth spending some times to consider the facets
              you will show to your customers. I've seen a site
              (Luckily, not using Episerver Commerce), to list al-
              most every size of the smartphones they have: 1.2",
              1.7", 2.3", 3.0", 3.5", 3.7", 4", etc etc. I consider those to
              be a bad facet configuration. Nowadays, you should
              only have something like "Less than 4"", "From 4"-
              5.1"", "From 5.2" to 6"", and "More than 6"" (more or
              less, you can argue about the exact numbers, but you
              get the idea). As a developer, you might not be the
              one to decide which facets will be, but if you see some
              bad facets like above, make sure to bring some good
              arguments and make your facets better.

All the SearchFilter(s) configured will be available in
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs    219

1 SearchFilterHelper.Current.SearchConfig.SearchFilters

    At first, we can try to add all the filters:

1 foreach (SearchFilter filter in SearchConfig.SearchFilter\

2 s)

3{

4      criteria.Add(filter);

5}

    This signals the search provider to return the matching count of
    all facets, and you can display them in your fancy shiny website.
    When a customer select a facet (which is commonly stored in the
    URI QueryString), we can try to include that filter into the search.
    First, we need to figure out which facets have been selected:

1 var filters = new List<SelectedFilter>();

2 foreach (string key in Request.QueryString.AllKeys)

3{

4      foreach (var filter in SearchFilterHelper.Current.Sea\

5 rchConfig.SearchFilters)

6      {

7         //Get the values from QueryString, here we are as\

8 suming | is the separator

9         var values = Request.QueryString[filter.field].Sp\

10 lit('|');

11        foreach (var value in values)

12        {

13            if (filter.Values.SimpleValue != null)

14            {

15               var filterValue = filter.Values.SimpleVal\

16 ue

17               .FirstOrDefault(x => x.value.Equals(v\

18 alue, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));

19               if (filterValue != null)
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs     220

20               {

21                   filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter\

22 , filterValue));

23               }

24            }

25

26            if (filter.Values.RangeValue != null)

27            {

28               var filterValue = filter.Values.RangeValue

29                   .FirstOrDefault(x => x.key.Equals(val\

30 ue, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));

31               if (filterValue != null)

32               {

33                   filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter\

34 , filterValue));

35               }

36            }

37

38            if (filter.Values.PriceRangeValue != null)

39            {

40               var filterValue = filter.Values.PriceRang\

41 eValue

42                   .FirstOrDefault(x => x.key.Equals(val\

43 ue, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase)

44                   && x.currency.Equals(SiteContext.Curr\

45 ent.Currency.CurrencyCode,

46                   StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));

47               if (filterValue != null)

48               {

49                   filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter\

50 , filterValue));

51               }

52            }

53         }

54  }
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  221

55 }
56 return filters;

     The idea is simpler than the code looks (the nested foreach:es and
     if:s make it looks worse) we iterate over all the keys in QueryString
     (split by "|", you can of course choose another delimiter):

    · If a key is found to be a filter.field, we get its value and
      split it into values(Assuming you want to allow multiple
      facets selection, for example, filter for both "red" and "blue").

    · If a value matches one of the Values of the filter, we add
      a new SelectedFilter to the list. Depends on the type of
      filter.Values:

         ­ SimpleValue, the value must be matching a simple field.

         ­ RangeValue, the value must be within the selected range,
            such as size.

         ­ PriceRangeValue, the price (in the currently selected
            currency) must be within the selected range.

    · After all the keys were tested, we return a list of selected
      filters.

    The last step is to include the selected filters in your search criteria,
    to filter the products customer wanted to see:

1 foreach (var filter in filters)

2{

3   if (filter.PriceRangeValue != null)

4   {

5      criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.PriceRan\

6 geValue);

7   }

8   if (filter.RangeValue != null)

9   {

10     criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.RangeVal\
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  222

11 ue);

12    }

13    if (filter.SimpleValue != null)

14    {

15        criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.SimpleVa\

16 lue);

17    }

18 }

    And then we can pass this criteria to the search APIs. It's easy
    after all, right?

                        Search provider internals.

    Prior to Commerce R3a, catalog entries must be indexed manually.
    That means, every time you edit an entry, you must trigger an
    index build, so the newly updated fields will be search-able. This
    can be done by manually clicking on the "Build index" button
    in Commerce Manager, or to run the "Incremental Search Index"
    scheduled job. Of course that is not something you would like to.
    Commerce R3a came with a feature allow the indexing to be fully
    automated - you are no longer required to go to Search Index and
    click the Build Index button manually.

    Search Index UI in Commerce Manager. However, the status of the processor
    is jokingly called "The most mysterious feature in Commerce Manager"

    The indexer handle all three scenarios:
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  223

· Rebuild the index entirely: which does mean all entries will be
  re-indexed, regardless if they have been changed since the last
  indexing time. This is an expensive operation, of course, and
  you should not use it for a running production site. However,
  it might make senses to do that when you deploy your site,
  when the content types (and the the metaclasses) of your
  entries might have changed (metafields have been added or
  removed). And that is not "Recorded" by the system.

· Build the index: SearchProvider system keeps track of the
  last time index was built, and it finds out and tries to index
  the entries which have been updated since - aka incremental
  indexing. This is the preferred option as it's reasonable cheap,
  however, it (theoretically) should not be needed anymore, if
  you enable...

· Index by event: The indexer will try to index every time an
  entry is updated. This was added in Commerce R3, and it has
  been working pretty well, except when it's not. We'll dive
  deep to see how it works and how to make it works a bit
  later.

              It's important to know that it's not only the entries
              which were actually changed. The SearchProvider
              system has to take the relation into account as well. So
              if you move a node to a new location, all of its entries
             - either true children ("Primary" node) or linked will
              need to be reindexed.

SearchProviders are configured by Mediachase.Search.config, in
Configs folder of both front-end and Commerce Manager site,
here's an example of how it looks like:
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs     224

1 <?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>

2 <Mediachase.Search>

3 <SearchProviders defaultProvider="LuceneSearchProvider">

4   <providers>

5   <add name="Solr35SearchProvider" type="Mediachase.S\

6 earch.Providers.Solr35.SolrSearchProvider, Mediachase.Sea\

7 rch.Solr35SearchProvider" queryBuilderType="Mediachase.Se\

8 arch.Providers.Solr35.SolrSearchQueryBuilder, Mediachase.\

9 Search.Solr35SearchProvider" url="http://localhost:8080/s\

10 olr" shareCores="true" facetLocalizedFieldValuesOnly="tru\

11 e" commitWithin="10000" maximumBatchSize="50" />

12

13  <add name="LuceneSearchProvider" type="Mediachase.S\

14 earch.Providers.Lucene.LuceneSearchProvider, Mediachase.S\

15 earch.LuceneSearchProvider" queryBuilderType="Mediachase.\

16 Search.Providers.Lucene.LuceneSearchQueryBuilder, Mediach\

17 ase.Search.LuceneSearchProvider" storage="<path-to-index-\

18 folder>" simulateFaceting="true" />

19

20  <add name="FindSearchProvider" type="EPiServer.Comm\

21 erce.FindSearchProvider.FindSearchProvider, EPiServer.Com\

22 merce.FindSearchProvider" serviceUrl="http://<find-index-\

23 url>" defaultIndex="myindex" />

24  </providers>

25  </SearchProviders>

26  <Indexers basePath="<path-to-index-folder>">

27  <add name="catalog" type="Mediachase.Search.Extension\

28 s.Indexers.CatalogIndexBuilder, Mediachase.Search.Extensi\

29 ons" />

30 </Indexers>

31 </Mediachase.Search>

    There are a couple of things you want to care about:

    · The defaultProvider - this is the search provider
      you're going to use. The required attribute is the
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  225

type, because Mediachase.Search needs to create an

instance of it, other attributes then will be used by the

search provider. The configured type must inherit from

Mediachase.Search.SearchProvider.

· The basePath - this is the path to store the indices, it's

only used by file-based indexers, in most of the case cases,

LuceneSearchProvider. HTTP-based indexers, such as

Solr35SearchProvider or FindSearchProvider, do not care

about this setting. There one thing to remember if you

are using file-based indexers: make sure the paths in both

Mediachase.Search.config point to same directory, and both

AppPool for both frontend site and Commerce Manage have

correct access to this folder. They should have at least read

access, and if you want to index on that site, you would need

to give delete/write access.

· The type of Indexer. This is where you register how

your providers index the entries, it must implement

ISearchIndexBuilder interface. For the first party

LuceneSearchProvider          and                  Solr35SearchProvider,

it's CatalogIndexBuilder, which inherits from

BaseCatalogIndexBuilder. Normally if you want to

implement your own Indexer, this class will be very

important.

When the indexing is performed, BaseCatalogIndexBuilder calls to
this method

1 protected virtual int IndexCatalogEntryDto(IndexBuilder i\
2 ndexer, CatalogEntryDto.CatalogEntryRow entryRow, string \
3 defaultCurrency, string[] languages)

    IndexCatalogEntryDto creates an ISearchDocument from the
    entryRow, and then finally call to this method in the provider:
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  226

1 public override void Index(string applicationName, string\
2 scope, ISearchDocument document)

    FindSearchProvider works a bit different, it registers
    FindSearchIndexBuilder, which overrides IndexCatalogEntryDto
    directly to create ISearchDocument in its own way, but at the end
    it also calls to SearchProvider.Index to do the index.

              Implements a search provider yourself:

    The search system allows you to create your own search provider.
    There are three steps you can do:

        · Implement your own search provider. This
           can be done by creating a class inherits from

Again, you don't have to override all of these. You can inherit
from BaseCatalogIndexBuilder and override only BuildIndex and
UpdateIndex. Or you can override only how each entry is indexed,
by overriding IndexCatalogEntryDto.

      · Register your implementations as the defaultSearchProvider
        and the indexer.

              Demystifying the index processor

With proper configuration, Optimizely Commerce will index the
changed entries automatically, without your manual interactions.
To allow this, Commerce relies on the ChangeNotification concept
from the CMS Core framework. In short, it's a database-backed
queue. When one or more entries are updated, Commerce will
send those updates to the queue. That also happens when a node is
updated, a node-entry relation or a catalog association is updated,
all related entries will be sent to the queue.

      If you wonder what is actually sent to the queue - it's
      the code of the entry. As we learned from Part 1, Code
      is unique across the system and can be used to identify
      the entry.

Meanwhile, Commerce has one class registered to
process such change - CatalogEntryChangeProcessor.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  230

ChangeNotificationManager will call CatalogEntryChangeProcessor
when it finds anything in the queue that CatalogEntryChangeProcessor
registered to. By default, every 5 seconds (a "heartbeat"),
ChangeNotificationManager will get remaining items from the
queues (It will get the Code, then use ICatalogSystem to get the
CatalogEntryDto) and call the processors to process those.

There are several statuses of the processor:

    · Valid: the processor is working and it'll update the indices
      when there are changes. This is of course the status you want
      to see.

    · Invalid: This is the status when you click on Invalidate button.
      That means you don't want the processor to continue process-
      ing the queue. You'll be able to build the index manually.

    · Recovering: It's usually when the status of the processor when
      you click "Recover" when it was "Invalid". The processor is
      trying to recover consistency - and by that does mean it is
      rebuilding the index.

            This can take quite long time, depending on your catalogs

    · Closed: This is the status you don't want to see - normally
      it means something went totally wrong, and the processor is
      no longer to process the queue. Your only option is usually to
      restart your site.

    · Opening: This usually happens at time the site starts, it's so
      fast that you hardly see the processor in this state.

The automatic indexing processor is a great feature, well, at least in
theory. It works really well if you have small, frequent updates. Not
so much if you have big chunk of updates, frequently. However, it
comes with a fairly big con - it's not that stable. It is possible to
become Closed once in a while, and no really knows what is wrong
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs         231

[^foo5020]. As I said, it requires a site restart to make it works
again. Your better/safer option is to use the incremental indexing. If
you have small, frequent updates, you can set up the "Incremental
indexing job" to run automatically every 5, 10, or 15 minutes.

              One of the reason that you might consider the even-
              tual indexing is that is faster. The incremental index-
              ing can be slow in specific scenarios. Performance-
              wise, it can be quite problematic if you have a big
              catalog, many relations between node and entries,
              and a very big log - time to get the . Worry no more,
              Commerce 11.4 comes with a new queuing system,
              which would make that problem goes away.

I feel the obligation to tell you this: No I don't like
having that problem hanging around version after
version. But sometimes it's something you have to
accept the reality. There are "Heisenbug" that you
can't really trace it, debug it, or fix it. The priority
is not just there. I tried, but I just didn't succeed.

                Search by Search API:s

In previous section we talked about the index-based search, which
should be great choice for implementing search features. But
SearchProviders are not without limitations: they are not real-time,
and they are read-only. When you need to update data, or if you
need strictly up-to-date information, you might need to Search
API:s

Episerver Commerce provides a SQL-based API:s for you to search
for entries which match a certain criteria. They works in real-time
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  232

without any delay like the solution provided by Search Providers,
one data is committed, it is immediately searchable.

But they are not that fast. Because they hit database every time,
and in many, to query on a large set of data. Search API:s are
mostly used in Commerce Manager, and you can use them for some
batching updates, but they definitely should not be used in any
customer-facing features. For such features, use Search providers
instead.

              I would even say that if you are building search
              features that end users can access via Search API:s,
              you are probably doing it wrong.

     Catalog Batch Update is one example of how Search API:s can be used

Search API:s are built around CatalogSearchParameters and
CatalogSearchOptions. Here's the simplest example of using
Search API:s
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs   233

1 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();
2 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchP\
3 arameters();
4 int totalCount = 0;
5 CatalogEntryDto entryDto = catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(sea\
6 rchParams, options, ref totalCount);

    In case you don't specify CatalogSearchOptions.RecordsToRetrieve,
    entryDto will contain every entry in the system - and you don't
    want to use such code in your production system. A more realistic
    approach is to iterate over variations, so we can process in batches.
    This is a snippet to load and process every 200 entries per batch:

1 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();

2 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchP\

3 arameters();

4 int totalCount = 0;

5 options.Classes.Add("Variant");

6 //We don't want to get any entry, we only need the totalC\

7 ount

8 options.RecordsToRetrieve = 0;

9 catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(searchParams, options, ref tot\

10 alCount);

11 int recordsCount = 0;

12 while (totalCount > 0 && recordsCount < totalCount)

13 {

14    options.RecordsToRetrieve = 200;

15    options.StartingRecord = recordsCount;

16    var catalogEntriesDto = CatalogContext.Current.FindIt\

17 emsDto(searchParams, options, ref totalCount, new Catalog\

18 EntryResponseGroup(CatalogEntryResponseGroup.ResponseGrou\

19 p.Info));

20    //Do some processing here

21    recordsCount += 200;

22 }
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs             234

          There is no "best" value for batch size. Speaking from
          experience, you can choose anything between 100 and
          500 for batch size. Smaller or bigger sizes can have
          negative impacts on performance, but your mileage
          might vary.

    CatalogSearchOptions contains settings of how the search should
    behave - which classes to search for, how many rows should be
    returned, which is the start row to get, or should the cache result be
    cached. CatalogSearchOptions inherits from SearchOptions with
    the Namespace is set to "Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog":

13        /// <summary>

14        /// The start record to return.

15        /// </summary>

18        /// <summary>

19        /// Indicates if the search should return total c\

    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs    235

29       /// <summary>

30       /// Indicates if the results should be cached.

31       /// </summary>

32       public bool CacheResults { get; set; }

34       /// <summary>

36       /// </summary>

39       ...

40  }

    CatalogSearchParameters contains some filter you can apply to
    your search:

1 public sealed class CatalogSearchParameters : SearchParam\

2 eters

3   {

4        /// <summary>

5        /// Filter by catalog names.

6        /// </summary>

7        public StringCollection CatalogNames { get; }

    If you recall about CatalogEntrySearchCriteria, you can see the
    similarity here - same properties for filtering results. Personally
    I think this is a place where Mediachase eCF got it right - the
    consistency in API:s is very important to make them easy to
    remember and use. However, if you want to expose the true powers
    of Search API:s, you would have to look into the base class -
    SearchParameters.

1 public abstract class SearchParameters

2   {

3         /// <summary>

4         /// Gets or sets the type of the join used in the\

5 query.

6         /// The example types are: inner join, outer join\

7 , left join and right join.

9         public string JoinType { get; set; }

10

11        /// <summary>

12        /// Gets or sets the join source table. This valu\
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs        237

13 e has to be a table name that exists in original query.

14           /// </summary>

15           public string JoinSourceTable { get; set; }

16

17           /// <summary>

18           /// Gets or sets the join target query. This can \

34           /// Gets or sets the SQL where clause. You can sp\

35 ecify filter conditions against the tables returned by th\

36 e query.

37           /// </summary>

39

41           /// Gets or sets the SQL meta where clause.

42           /// </summary>

52     /// </summary>

54  }

    Don't be overwhelmed by these properties. You will most likely use
    two properties - SqlWhereClause and SqlMetaWhereClause. They are
    extremely flexible and powerful, but can also be confusing at times.
    Simply put, SqlWhereClause can be used when you want to filter on
    CatalogEntry or CatalogNode table, while SqlMetaWhereClause can
    be used when you you want to filter on the metafields.

    To understand how they can be used, let's try an example: find
    entries which are going to expire today. We know that the expire
    date is represented by EndDate in CatalogEntry table, so we need
    to have a filter on SqlWhereClause:

1 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();
2 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchP\
3 arameters();
4 searchParams.SqlWhereClause = " [CatalogEntry].[EndDate] \
5 < " + DateTime.UtcNow.ToString("yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss");
6 int totalCount = 0;
7 CatalogEntryDto entryDto = catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(sea\
8 rchParams, options, ref totalCount);

    Simple, right? You can certainly extend this to find entries which
    are going to expire next week or next month.

       It's worth noting that when you work with database,
       you should always use UTC timezone. All DateTime
       values are supposed to be saved as UTC in Commerce
       database.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs   239

Let's take another example - let's say we want to find
the entries belong to a specific metaclass. With the
CatalogSearchOptions.Classes we can filter Product, Variant,
Package or Bundle, but we might want to go further and filter for
FashionSKU or SLR. Again, SqlWhereClause can solve this because
CatalogEntry has a column named MetaClassId which indicates
the metaclass of the entry.

 1 //Assuming we don't know the Id, but only the name of the\
 2 MetaClass
 3 MetaClass metaClass = MetaClass.Load(CatalogContext.MetaD\
 4 ataContext, "DSLR");
 5 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();
 6 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchP\
 7 arameters();
 8 searchParams.SqlWhereClause = " [CatalogEntry].[MetaClass\
 9 Id] = " + metaClass.Id.ToString();
10 int totalCount = 0;
11 CatalogEntryDto entryDto = catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(sea\
12 rchParams, options, ref totalCount);

     Of course, you can combine those condition in one query - you can
     find entries belong to "DSLR" metaclass AND expire next week.

You can't, however, search for entries belong to
"DSLR" metaclass OR expire next week in one query.
You would need to search for both and then combine
the result.

While SqlWhereClause is quite straightforward, SqlMetaWhereClause
can be a bit tricky to work with. The idea is still the same - to filter
by SQL. If you recall from how metaclasses are stored in the first
chapter, you can see that the tables for metaclasses are dropped
in Commerce 9, and replaced by the views. The views act pretty
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  240

     much the same way, but due to their structure, the query will be a
     bit different.

     Let's go through another example: find entries which "Color"
     property, with type LongString has value of "Blue"

 1 MetaClass metaClass = MetaClass.Load(CatalogContext.MetaD\
 2 ataContext, "SLR");
 3 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();
 4 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchP\
 5 arameters();
 6 searchParams.SqlMetaWhereClause = " META.MetaFieldName = \
 7 'Color' AND META.LongString = 'Blue'";
 8 int totalCount = 0;
 9 CatalogEntryDto entryDto = catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(sea\
10 rchParams, options, ref totalCount);

     So you need to specify field name, by `META.MetaFieldName', and
     field value, by `META.<field type>'.

                    The "new" META.MetaFieldName pattern is introduced
                    in Commerce 9. If you forgot, the way catalog data
                    is stored was significantly changed in Commerce 9.
                    For most parts, that was for the better. However,
                    when it comes to search for entries, it is a drawback.
                    Previously you can search for entries which matches
                    two or more conditions, for example, by using this
                    query META.Color = 'RED' AND META.Size = 'Large'.
                    However, this will not possible in the new pattern.
                    You can only search for entries which match one con-
                    dition in one query. If you want to filter by both, you
                    would need two queries and then intersect the result.
                    This was acknowledged as a known issue, but will not
                    be fixed, because fixing it would mean introducing
                    another breaking change.

SqlMetaWhereClause and SqlWhereClause are not exclusive - they
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  241

can be used in conjunction to create really powerful queries which
combine from various condition. You can use the previous example
to find entries which belongs to T-shirt metaclass, and have red
color.

              It's important to remember that Search API:s do not
              know about, or work with versioning. They can only
              work with the published data (which are represented
              by CatalogEntry, CatalogNode and the views repre-
              sent content properties). Currently there are no API:s
              to find data from draft versions, and there probably
              will be no such features.

                     Find Commerce

Optimizely Search & Navigation, formerly Episerver Find, in term
of client, is a .NET API:s for ElasticSearch, in term of service, it's
an indexing service built on ElasticSearch. Its arguably strongest
point is support for strongly typed types, so you can write complex
queries which are also easy to understand. As a hosted service,
you will need to buy your subscription for Search & Navigation
- depends on how many items you want to index, and how big your
traffic, there are multiple packages for you. If you are using Op-
timizely DXP however, a subscription is included (and is possible
to upgrade). In scope of this book, we will still use the Find term,
because it's shorter and it matches with actual APIs.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs     242

Find Commerce vs FindSearchProvider:
FindSearchProvider is just a wrapper to allow
entries to be indexed and searched by Find. It does
not leverage the true power of Find, or content in a
"proper way". The advantage of FindSearchProvider
is it can be used for older versions of Commerce,
including R1 SP2 and R3, and it can be used in
Commerce Manager. In terms of features and
flexibility, FindSearchProvider can't not compete
to Find Commerce, it's not even close. If you are
developing a new site, and intend to use Episerver
Find, then Find Commerce should be your first
choice. FindCommerce can only work on front-end
site, however this still makes it a great solution
because that's where you have to deal with the most
complicated scenarios and demanding loads. This is
especially true with Commerce 14.0 and newer when
Commerce Manager is considered dead.

              Find Commerce comes with a separated nuget pack-
              age, EPiServer.Commerce.Find, and it is not released
              in Commerce "bundle". It has its own version and
              release schedule.

Find Commerce, as Find itself, relies heavily on extension methods
and lambda expressions. Are you ready to go?

                Find Commerce configurations

Find Commerce comes with many configurations, and most of
them can be done by code. If we are to override some configura-
tions, it's best to do that in an IInitializableModule which has
dependency on FindCommerceInitializationModule. This way, we
can be sure to override the default configuration and dependency
registrations.
   Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  243

1 [InitializableModule]
2 [ModuleDependency(typeof(EPiServer.Find.Commerce.FindComm\
3 erceInitializationModule))]
4 public class MyFindCommerceInitializationModule : IInitia\
5 lizableModule
6{

7
8}

    By default, Find Commerce will re-index the content every time
    its prices or it inventories are updated. Inventories can be updated
    often (especially if your goods are being sold like hot cakes), it might
    make senses to disable re-indexing on inventory updated events.
    How?

    Find Commerce uses CatalogContentEventListener to handle pric-
    ing and inventory events. This listener will do a check to see if
    it should index a content when an inventory event triggers - if the
    content type was configured to index the inventories. So this is how
    Find Commerce configures that:

1 protected virtual void ApplyIStockPlacementConventions()

2{

3   SearchClient.Instance.Conventions.ForInstancesOf<ISto\

4 ckPlacement>()

5   .ExcludeField(c => c.InventoryReference)

6   .IncludeField(x => x.Inventories());

7}

   The "conventions" are built around SearchClient.Instance.Conventions.
   Here we are saying: for contents implement IStockPlacement,
   exclude InventoryReference, but include Inventories() in the
   index. If we want to exclude Inventories from the index, we need
   to override it
   Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs    244

1 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

2{

3   SearchClient.Instance.Conventions.ForInstancesOf<ISto\

4 ckPlacement>().ExcludeField(c => c.Inventories());

5}

   because our IInitializableModule runs after FindCommerceInitializationModule,
   this will override the default conventions. Our site no longer indices
   the contents when an inventory event triggers. Basically, we can
   also override any default convention with this method.

   Now consider another scenario. We want to index the content with
   the inventory information. But to reduce the overload, we only
   index if the the SKU has less than 10 items in stock (if it is more
   than 10, just display something like "10+ in stock"). First, we need to
   unhook the default event listener in CatalogContentEventListener:

1 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

2{

3   _broadcaster = context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<Ca\

4 talogKeyEventBroadcaster>();

5   _listener = context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<Catal\

6 ogContentEventListener>();

7   _broadcaster.InventoryUpdated -= _listener.InventoryU\

8 pdated;

9}

   Then we need to register our custom listener. As we talked in
   Part 4 - Events, the sender contains list of InventoryRecords, while
   InventoryUpdateEventArgs contains list of CatalogKeys, Name of the
   event and a flag for ApplicationHasContentModelTypes. Here we
   need to check if the remaining stock in all warehouses of a item is
   less than 10 or not.
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  245

1 private void InventoryUpdated(object sender, InventoryUpd\

2 ateEventArgs args)

3{

4     var inventoryRecords = sender as IEnumerable<Inventor\

5 yRecord>;

6     if (inventoryRecords == null)

7     {

8        return;

9     }

10

11    var inventoryGroups = inventoryRecords.GroupBy(c => c\

12 .CatalogEntryCode);

13    foreach (var inventoryGroup in inventoryGroups)

14    {

15       if (inventoryGroup.Sum(c=>c.PurchaseAvailableQuan\

16 tity) < 10m)

17       {

18           _contentEventIndexer.SavingContent(_reference\

19 Converter.GetContentLink(inventoryGroup.Key, CatalogConte\

20 ntType.CatalogEntry));

21       }

22    }

23 }

    _contentEventIndexer is an instance of ContentEventIndexer,
    the class used for indexing contents in Find, and that's the
    simple way to index a content - calling SavingContent with the
    ContentReference of the content to be indexed. Still remember our
    little buddy ReferenceConverter?

                      Search with Find Commerce

    If you have experience with Find already, this should be easy. But
    if you don't, let's break down a normal search:
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs    246

1 var variationContentSearch = SearchClient

2   .Instance

3   .Search<VariationContent>()

4   .For(searchText)

5   .FilterPriceAvailableForCurrentUser()

6   .FilterForVisitor()

7   .Select(x => new

8   {

9       x.ContentLink,

10      Inventory = ((IStockPlacement)x).Inventories(),

11      Prices = ((IPricing)x).Prices(),

12

13      ImageUrl = x.DefaultImageUrl(),

14      x.DisplayName,

15      Description = x.Description(),

16      x.Code

17  })

18  .ApplyBestBets()

19  .Track()

20  .Skip(startIndex)

21  .Take(pageSize)

22  .GetResult();

    "Woah woah, wait a minute" - I can hear you. I can understand, this
    can look scary at first. But not after we go through it one by one:

    · We are using SearchClient.Instance, this might look strange
      to you, but it's acceptable for Find.

    · We are searching for VariationContent, so no
      NodeContentBase, or even ProductContent. Find is all
      about strongly types, so if you want to search for "TV" - and
      you have a content type for that, simply change the type to
      "TVContent".

    · A matching searchText is expected.
    · We only search for entries which have prices available for

      current user.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  247

· We only search for entries which are available for current
  user. So if an entry is only available in "US" Market, but the
  current user is in "Europe" Market, the entry will be filtered
  out.

· We don't want the entire content in the search result, we
  only want some important fields, the ContentLink, prices,
  inventories, the image, display name, etc. Again, it's about
  strongly typed types - so if your content has a property named
  "ScreenSize", and it's indexed, it can be used here.

· We are applying the "BestBets" here. It's a feature in Find
  that allows you to link a specific content to a keyword. For
  example, if you know customers often make a typo searching
  for "ASpecailKeyword", instead of "ASpecialKeyword", you
  can configure that keyword for a content which should be in
  the search result. Or if you know customers often search for
  a keyword that your products do not contain, but you want
  to promote one anyway.

                           An example of best bets in Find UI

    · We want to track the search. For analytics, of course.
    · We are using paging technique here. Note that Skip and Take

      here are not LINQ, although they look the same. Note that
      Find query and LINQ expressions can't be used in conjunc-
      tion.
    · After building the query, we finally get the result. As most
      sites use Find as a cloud service (provided by Episerver itself),
      it's best to make the traffic as small as possible - so the way
      Find works is to only return what you need.

On your search page, you usually tweak the query above, but the
core concept is more or less the same.
   Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  248

                              Advanced filters

   The strongly typed types properties work a bit differently with
   "complex" (aka nested) types. Inventory and Prices are example
   of nested types, and you can have plenty of filtering scenarios for
   them in a Episerver Commerce site. For example, filtering entries
   which have prices in the current currency, or have prices less than
   a specific value. Find Commerce gets you covered - it provides a lot
   of filters for IPricing and IStockPlacement.

   For example, if we want to filter only variations which have
   (default) prices less than 100, in current currency, the above query
   can be rewritten like this:

1 var variationContentSearch = SearchClient

2  .Instance

3  .Search<VariationContent>()

4  .For(searchText)

5  .FilterPriceAvailableForCurrentUser()

6  .FilterForVisitor()

7  .Filter(p=>p.PriceLessThan(100m))

8  ...

   It's good that Find Commerce has better documentation about its
   filters than Find itself. It would still take a while for you to discover
   all the filters, and when to use what, but believe me, it's worth your
   time and effort.

   This section is not supposed to be your complete reference about
   Find Commerce, but instead a glimpse at it. I hope this crash course
   made you like Find Commerce - and Find in general. You now can
   explore it powers to make your coding days a joyful experience.
   Find Commerce is built on the foundation of Find which provides
   many features, such as Best Bet.
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  249

                          Indexing

When you have Find Commerce, one thing that you should consider
is if you frequently import big catalogs: performance. I will be frank
with you: Find Commerce will triple, or even more your importing
time. This is because how versions are get to indexed. Every time
an entry or a node is changed, Find Commerce will have to get
all published versions (in all languages), or if no published version
exists, the common draft version(s). This is indeed very expensive.

            Searching for orders

Searching for orders is another essential part of your website.
Finding for orders which are ready to release, or find a specific order
by a tracking number, are two examples that you will frequently do.
Let's see how we can do it.

Searching for orders shares many characteristics with searching
for entries. They even share a large portion of API:s. Order
searching API:s are built upon OrderSearchOptions and
OrderSearchParameters, which inherit from SearchOptions
and SearchParameters, respectively, so they have same base
classes as CatalogSearchOptions and CatalogSearchParameters.

OrderSearchOptions is almost identical with CatalogSearchOptions.
The only different is int the Namespace property - it limits the
search to `Mediachase.Commerce.Orders'. OrderSearchParameters
is an even more simple inheritance of SearchParameters: it does
not have any new properties on its own.

Searching for orders (including Cart, PaymentPlan and
PurchaseOrder) can be done with OrderContext class. This
class provides many methods to find orders, carts, and payment
plans. In the past, you have separated methods to find carts,
payment plan, or purchase orders. For example, to find for Cart,
we can use one of these methods:
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  250

 1 public Cart[] FindCarts(OrderSearchParameters parameters,\
 2 OrderSearchOptions options)
 3 public Cart[] FindCarts(OrderSearchParameters parameters,\
 4 OrderSearchOptions options, out int totalRecords)

     And similar method for PurchaseOrder and PaymentPlan. However,
     in later versions of Commerce, they have been consolidated into a
     few generic methods. Methods like above are now obsoleted and
     should be avoided. To find carts, here is how you do it:

 1 public T[] Search<T>(OrderSearchParameters parameters, Or\
 2 derSearchOptions options) where T : OrderGroup
 3 public T[] Search<T>(OrderSearchParameters parameters, Or\
 4 derSearchOptions options, out int totalRecords) where T :\
 5 OrderGroup

     Let's write a simple example of searching for carts - we want to
     delete the carts which are abandoned, customers added something
     to the carts but then left and forgot about it. There is already a
     scheduled job for this, but let's try to implement it to see what
     happens under the hood:

 1 int startIndex = 0;
 2 int totalRecords = 0;
 3 int daysOld = 30;
 4 OrderSearchParameters parameters = new OrderSearchParamet\
 5 ers();
 6 parameters.SqlMetaWhereClause = String.Format("Meta.Modif\
 7 ied < GETDATE() - {0}", daysOld.ToString());
 8 OrderSearchOptions options = new OrderSearchOptions();
 9 options.StartingRecord = startIndex;
10 OrderContext.Current.Search<Cart>(parameters, options, ou\
11 t totalRecords);
12 while (totalRecords > 0)
13 {
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  251

14    Cart[] carts = OrderContext.Current.Search<Cart>(para\

15 meters, options, out totalRecords);

16    //Delete the carts

17 }

                    Just like the example to load entries we had before -
                    we are loading and processing carts in batches here. In
                    most of the cases, it's better to load and process entries
                    and orders in small chunks: loading one by one is a
                    waste, while loading everything at once can result in
                    timeout or out of memory exception.

     It looks similar, right? The only difference with the sample code
     we had with searching for entries is we use different class "Shop-
     pingCart". You might wonder where the "Meta.Modified" field
     comes from. Cart, PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder are metaclasses
     extend OrderGroup - if you still remember the concepts from Order
     system chapter. When you want to filter on a field specific to
     one of these class, you have to filter with "Meta.<FieldName>"
     on SqlMetaWhereClass statement, otherwise you filter on "[Order-
     Group].<FieldName>" on SqlWhereClause. Let's look at another
     example where we search for wish lists, where we do filter on the
     [OrderGroup].Name:

 1 int startIndex = 0;
 2 int totalRecords = 0;
 3 int totalCount = 0;
 4 OrderSearchParameters parameters = new OrderSearchParamet\
 5 ers();
 6 parameters.SqlWhereClause = "[OrderGroup].Name = 'WishLis\
 7 t'";
 8 OrderSearchOptions options = new OrderSearchOptions();
 9 options.Classes.Add("ShoppingCart");
10 options.StartingRecord = startIndex;
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs     252

11 OrderContext.Current.Search<Cart>(parameters, options, ou\

12 t totalRecords);

13 while (totalRecords > totalCount)

14 {

15    Cart[] carts = OrderContext.Current.Search<Cart>(para\

16 meters, options, out totalRecords);

17    totalCount += 20;

18    //Do something with the wish lists

19 }

    SqlWhereClause is just a normal WHERE clause, where you can
    combine conditions, such as AND and OR to form complex query.
    In above example, we only filter by Name, but with can combine
    with other conditions, such as MarketId. Above methods could be
    used to search for PaymentPlan as well. Using SqlMetaWhereClause,
    you can search for the cycle, for example, this code will search for
    PaymentPlans with the cycle of 2 weeks:

1 int startIndex = 0;

2 int totalRecords = 0;

3 int totalCount = 0;

4 OrderSearchParameters parameters = new OrderSearchParamet\

5 ers();

6 parameters.SqlMetaWhereClause = "[META].PlanCycleMode = 2\

7 AND [META].PlanCycleLength = 2";

8 OrderSearchOptions options = new OrderSearchOptions();

9 options.Classes.Add("PaymentPlan");

10 options.StartingRecord = startIndex;

11 OrderContext.Current.Search<PaymentPlan>(parameters, opti\

12 ons, out totalRecords);

13 while (totalRecords > totalCount)

14 {

15    PaymentPlan[] paymentPlans = OrderContext.Current.Sea\

16 rch<PaymentPlan>(parameters, options, out totalRecords);

17    totalCount += 20;
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  253

18    //Do something with the payment plans

19 }

    And so on and so forth.

    Enough with the simple cases - let's try a more complicated case.
    How's about finding purchase orders with was made with lineitems
    which has a specific value of a custom metafield?

1 OrderSearchParameters parameters = new OrderSearchParamet\

2 ers();

3 parameters.SqlMetaWhereClause = "META.Color = 'Green'";

4 parameters.SqlWhereClause = "OrderGroupId IN (Select Obje\

5 ctId FROM OrderGroup_PurchaseOrder)";

6 OrderSearchOptions options = new OrderSearchOptions();

7 options.Classes.Add("LineItemEx");

8 PurchaseOrder[] purchaseOrders = OrderContext.Current.Sea\

9 rch<PurchaseOrder>(parameters, options);

    This is where the complicated stuffs kick in - to search for a
    metafield value, you'll need to search in LineItemEx by adding it
    to the Classes of OrderSearchOptions - then you would be able to
    query by SqlMetaWhereClause as normal with META.<FieldName>.
    The only catch here is that you'll have to make sure you're
    finding PurchaseOrder, not anything else. That's why we need the
    SqlWhereClause to limit the search.

    If you want to search for purchase orders which have a specific
    lineitem by code - unfortunately you can't apply the solution
    above. The problem is, the code belongs to LineItem, which is
    a system metaclass, while OrderSearchOptions.Classes can only
    work with user metaclasses (OrderGroup is a system metaclass,
    while PurchaseOrder, ShoppingCart and PaymentPlan are not). Here
    you will need to write a quite complex SQL query:
   Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  254

1 parameters.SqlWhereClause = "OrderGroupId IN (SELECT li.O\
2 rderGroupId From LineItem li WHERE li.Code = 'Pro-Episerv\
3 er-Commerce')";

    Truth is, Order-search API:s are really powerful - you can do almost
    anything to find the orders you want - however it's less than
    pleasant to test if your code is correct. I would suggest you to try the
    simple tool here¹ to verify your query and test the results almost
    immediately.

                     Search for serializable carts.

   As we discussed in the serializable cart chapter, searching for
   serializable cart is ... difficult. Yes you can search for certain fields,
   but if you want to search for any carts that contain a specific line
   item, it will be a dead end.

   There is an abstraction to find orders, IOrderSearchService, but it
   is limited by the underlying APIs.

1 public interface IOrderSearchService

2{

3     OrderSearchResults<TOrderGroup> Find<TOrderGroup>(Ord\

4 erSearchFilter filter) where TOrderGroup : class, IOrderG\

5 roup;

6

7     OrderSearchResults<TOrderGroup> Search<TOrderGroup>(O\

8 rderSearchField searchField, string searchValue, int star\

9 tIndex, int recordsToRetrieve) where TOrderGroup : class,\

10 IOrderGroup;

11 }

                This is one little weird interface. It has both "Find"
                and "Search" methods, with two different set of pa-
                rameters.

      ¹http://world.episerver.com/blogs/Quan-Mai/Dates/2014/10/Order-searchmade-easy/
Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  255

            Performance considerations

If you have many orders (or carts), and as we all know performance
is critical to any eCommerce websites to increase your conversion
rates), then finding orders via SearchAPI has something to consider
about.

First of all, not APIs are created equal. Some are faster, and some
are inevitably slower. OrderContext.FindPurchaseOrdersByStatus
is an example of the latter. It queries on a non-indexed column, and
even if you add the index, it does not help much (The Status column
has very little distinctive values, that would make a bad index). So
every time you call this method, it will (most likely) do an Clustered
Index Scan, which is both expensive, slow, and prone to deadlocks.
Be careful when you use this method - don't call it too much, and
don't call it concurrently. You might never need it, but I have seen
cases when this causes big troubles.

Second - you are going to add your metafields to your order classes
- PurchaseOrder, OrderForm, Shipment, LineItem - and chances are
you are going to query by them. For example, you might add a
metafield named PaymentReference to your PurchaseOrder, to track
the payment from the payment provider, and you want to get the
order from that PaymentReference. It would make senses to add a
non clustered index for that column, if it makes senses.

              An index makes senses if the values are highly selec-
              tive, and you frequently query by it. It's worth noting
              that an index is not a silver bullet, and adding it is not
              without drawbacks (Adding overhead to insert and
              delete, for example), so watch your database usage
              closely and decide if its advantages overweight the
              disadvantages.

Normally, you don't add index(es) to default/system metafields
- because it might interfere with the system index(es) (existing
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs  256

    or might be added in the future). However, there are exceptions
    (as always). One of the cases is the Created/Modified columns
    in OrderGroup_ShoppingCart, and OrderGroup_PurchaseOrder.
    Modified on OrderGroup_ShoppingCart will be used to find the
    "obsolete" carts (so you can remove them and keep your system
    clean), while Created in OrderGroup_PurchaseOrder will be used
    when you want to list the orders placed today, this week, this
    month, or this year. Those columns were considered as candidates
    for non clustered indices, but it was finally decided they should be
    added on a per site basis.

    Again, adding an index is trying to solve a problem, so only do that
    if you really have that problem. Don't try to solve a problem you
    don't have.

    It's also worth mentioning that, while you can use the overload
    which also returns the matching count of order, it's also more
    expensive than the other overload. If you have a lot of carts or
    orders in your system, the overhead can be significant. To avoid
    that, if the total count matters to you, it's best to run once to get it,
    then switch back to the other overload, for example:

1 _serializableCartDB.FindCarts(cartFilter, out var totalRe\

2 cords);

3 cartFilter.ReturnTotalCount = false;

4 cartFilter.RecordsToRetrieve = 100;

5

6 var cartLoaded = 0;

7 do

8{

9     var expiredCarts = _serializableCartDB.FindCarts(cart\

10 Filter).ToList();

11    foreach (var cart in expiredCarts)

12    {

13         _orderRespository.Delete(cart.OrderLink);

14         deletedCartsTotalCount++;
    Chapter 11: Search - where and how to find stuffs              257

15    }

16

17    OnStatusChanged($"Deleted {deletedCartsTotalCount} in\

18 {totalRecords} expired carts.");

19    cartLoaded = expiredCarts.Count;

20 }

21 while (cartLoaded > 0);

         True story, I had to introduce the overload that does
         not count the total record when we recognized a
         customer cannot use the "Remove Expired Carts"
         scheduled job to delete the old carts - because it's too
         slow. It improved performance significantly. But later,
         even that is not enough, when you have a few millions
         of carts in the database, the first query to count the
         carts is enough to time out. Then a direct query to
         delete carts was introduced, we don't count before
         hand, just report the number of carts deleted. Moral
         of the story: be adaptive and keep improving!
 Part 6: Promotions -
   Everybody loves
        discounts.

I always love sales off - buying something I like (even though might
not actually need) at a sweet, sweet discount, brings a lot of joy to
me. And I think that applies to a lot of people. Businesses runs that
way, marketing works that way and a good eCommerce site always
comes with discounts.
It might not be as good as working with discounts, as using them.
   Chapter 11: The new
     promotion system

           Marketing UI, the new editing UI for new promotion system

There are two terms being used by the new promotion system:
discount is the term in user-facing texts, and promotion is for API:s
level. In this chapter, we'll use promotion term.

                 Everything is content

When Episerver designed the new promotion system, one of the
first question need to be answered: How to work with data, includ-
ing persisting and editing. Several options were considered, in the
end, the content way won. There are several benefits of doing so:
the unified API:s (read, IContentRepository) can be used, and the
editing UI can be extended quite easily.
In new promotion system, everything is content.
Most of the concepts were carried from the old system, but adapted
to the content system. The structure of promotion contents is
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  260

more or less similar with other content system system - a root,
campaigns and promotions. A new CampaignRoot was introduced.
It's not a virtual content like the RootContent in Commerce, but
is saved to the database eventually. You can always access it by
SalesCampaignFolder.CampaignRoot.

Working with the new promotion means working with two
strongly content types - SalesCampaign and PromotionData, they
both reside in EPiServer.Commerce.Marketing namespace and
inherit from BasicContent. The structure of promotion contents is
much simpler than the structure of catalog contents: campaigns
are direct children of the Campaign root, and promotions
are direct children of campaigns. There is no other level, and
there are no direct relations between campaign-campaign or
promotion-promotion.

              Strictly speaking, there are indirect relations between
              promotion and promotion, as we'll find out later.

     Let's try out the content way to see how it works:

 1 var contentRepository = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstanc\
 2 e<IContentRepository>();
 3 var campaign = contentRepository.GetDefault<SalesCampaign\
 4 >(SalesCampaignFolder.CampaignRoot);
 5 campaign.Name = "Spring2022";
 6 campaign.ValidFrom = DateTime.Now;
 7 campaign.ValidUntil = DateTime.Now.AddMonths(1);
 8 campaign.IsActive = true;
 9 contentRepository.Save(campaign, DataAccess.SaveAction.Pu\
10 blish, EPiServer.Security.AccessLevel.NoAccess);

     I hope you can see the benefits of unified content API:s - you
     can read and understand the code without the explanation.
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  261

As usual, we get an instance of IContentRepository, then
create a default SalesCampaign instance as children of
SalesCampaignFolder.CampaignRoot (this is always required,
as we learned that SalesCampaign must be direct children of the
CampaignRoot), set some properties, and save it again.

                          We created a campaign by code. Yay!

SalesCampaign, PromotionData and their derivatives are handled
by the default ContentProvider (in other words, they are CMS
contents and are saved to CMS database instead of Commerce), so
theoretically, they can have versions. However, the Marketing UI
does not allow managing versions (You can't add any gadget other
than Catalog one). This might change in the future, but for now, you
should always use SaveAction.Publish with promotion contents.

              They do not support localizations as well. This is fre-
              quently requested as a campaign should be localized
              by the market. However, you can workaround that
              by using the Block in the promotions. The blocks
              themselves can be localized.

              Because campaigns and promotions are (just) CMS
              content, it is very simple to share those between your
              teams. Just export and import campaigns/promotions
              are pages. However, there are caveats with this ap-
              proach, linked items might not work.
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                262

SalesCampaign has information that can be useful for campaign
managers:

 1 - `Code`: can be used for tracking, such as Google Analyt\
 2 ics tracking code.
 3 - `VisitorGroups`: can be used to define groups of users \
 4 which are entitled to this campaign.
 5 - `TargetMarkets`: Only orders in these markets are entit\
 6 led to the promotions in this campaign. Empty market mean\
 7 s no orders can be redeemed, while set it to `EPiServer.B\
 8 usiness.Commerce.Constants.AllMarkets` ("EPI_AllMarkets")\
 9 means all markets. Note that this is added in a later ve\
10 rsion, before that you only have `TargetMarket`, which me\
11 an a campaign can only be set for only one market. If you\
12 are running a campaign in several markets, your only opt\
13 ion would be to duplicate that campaign for each market. \
14 For that reason, you should always keep your Optimizely C\
15 ommerce up to date with latest releases.
16 - `RevenueGoal`: This is more of report feature than actu\
17 ally being used in the system.

Of course, you can extend SalesCampaign to add the properties you
want to. However, in real world case scenarios, I don't think you,
as a developer, will work directly with SalesCampaign everyday.
It should be the work of editors/campaign managers to edit the
campaign in the UI. It's the PromotionData, the processors, and
the promotion engine we need to care about.
Chapter 11: The new promotion system                   264

As campaigns and promotions are content, there is an unexpected
benefit for that: you can import and export campaigns and promo-
tions like other content. Instead of having to synchronize database,
one merchandiser can create campaigns and promotions to test on
staging site, then export the change to a .episerverdata file, which
can be imported to the production site

           Export campaigns and promotions!

If you have references to catalog items in your pro-
motions, it's important to have same catalogs in both
staging and production sites. Catalog references are
stored as content guids, so they are consistent with
exports and imports. One caveat here is that shipping
promotion can't be simply exported and imported -
ShippingMethodId is Guid also but they are generated
every time a shipping method is created, it will be
different on each site.
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system     265

                    The promotion engine:

    The promotion engine can be considered as the core of new promo-
    tion engine. It's a very simple interface, with only two methods:

1 public interface IPromotionEngine

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Checks the condition for all active promotions, a\

5 nd gives awards for the promotions where the condition ha\

6 s been full filled.

7     /// </summary>

8     IEnumerable<RewardDescription> Run(IOrderGroup orderG\

9 roup);

10

11    /// <summary>

12    /// Checks the condition for all active promotions, a\

13 nd gives awards for the promotions where the condition ha\

14 s been

15    /// fulfilled. Promotions with higher priority (lower\

16 Priority value) will be evaluated first and all excluded\

17 promotions /// will not be evaluated.

18    /// </summary>

19    IEnumerable<RewardDescription> Run(IOrderGroup orderG\

20 roup, PromotionEngineSettings settings);

21 }

    For your convenience, PromotionEngine - the default implementa-
    tion will do those steps:

      · Loads all promotions available, then filters inactive one and
        sort them by priority.

      · Filters promotions which require coupon codes, but there was
        none supplied with the order group.

      · For each promotion:
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                266

         ­ Checks for redemption limits of promotion to see if a
            promotion can be fulfilled.

         ­ Loads the processor which can process the promotion,
            Evaluate it.

         ­ Each promotion will return a list of Redemption. Applies
            the redemptions until the redemption limits are reached.

    · Return list of RewardDescription. Each RewardDescription
      contains these information:

    is the Promotion types and the processors.

                  There is usually a confusion regarding ExcludedItems.
                  It might sound like "the items (products, SKUs) that

                  is actually the other promotions/campaigns that are
                  excluded (or exclude) this promotion. The naming
                  could have been better, but we stuck with it because
                  otherwise it'll be a major breaking change.

    Note that we don't have to inherit from PromotionData directly, we
    can inherit from its subclasses - EntryPromotion, OrderPromotion
    and ShippingPromotion, which basically PromotionData with

    and DiscountType.Shipping, respectively). There are three thing
