      · CouponData: By default, Coupon only contains one simple
        string field, where you can set the coupon code for the entire
Chapter 11: The new promotion system                   271

  promotion - more or less the same with the old system. We'll
  discuss later about extending this to support more advanced
  scenarios.

· RedemptionLimitsData: It can be used to limit how many
  times a promotion can be redeemed. You can set it to be per-
  promotion, per-orderform, or per-customer (Requires cus-
  tomers to be logged in). If multiple limits are applied, then the
  lowest available value will be used. Like PromotionSchedule,
  it's just a BlockData with three nullable int for the limits.

It is worth noting that it's extremely difficult to
cache the campaigns and promotions. For the same
request, sure, no problem. For a longer period, it'll
become problematic. As campaigns and promotions
get expired/become active all the time and there is
no mechanism for notifying such events, the cache
could become invalid without you knowing it. A
compromise is to cache them for a short amount of
time, such as 30 seconds or 1 minute. If a campaign
or promotion becomes expired or active during that
time, it is still acceptable.

                Promotion data and processor

                           Order-level promotion

Enough of theory, let try to implement one promotion for our own:
If a customer spend equal or more than a specific amount, he or she
will get a 10% discount off the cart. We will one to support multiple
currencies setting, for example, if the current cart is in USD, we
want the threshold to be $100, while if it is SEK, then the threshold
is SEK1000, etc...Because this cart affects the whole order (both the
conditions and rewards) then it should be an OrderPromotion. First,
let's create a content type for our promotion:
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                  272

1 [ContentType(GUID = "7660BDC2-6F65-4D22-8FDB-812EE7F4074D\

2 ")]

7 motion.

13     /// </summary>

14     public virtual decimal Discount { get; set; }

15 }

    The content type, as usual, has to be an unique GUID. The interest-
    ing here is Amounts property - which is an IList<Money>. This is one
    of two specially treated IList<T> properties in the Marketing UI -
    the other one is IList<ContentReference>. Build your project and
    then go to marketing view, you now can see our new promotion
    type, and creating it will give us something like this:

currency separately. Easy, right? We did almost nothing and there
are already results! However, there are two things to note: - The
list of currencies available depends on the market you choose for
your campaign. By default, "All" option is selected, which mean all
currencies from all markets will be available. Selecting a specific
market will limit the currencies you can set. - All the texts in new
promotion is only the type name, which is not really nice. You can
make it better by adding this localization file (for English, you can
add files for other languages in same structure) - as I said, it's just

                                    Localized promotion

The only thing remaining is to create a processor, which will be
called by the promotion engine to apply the promotion. We'll have
to inherit from PromotionProcessorBase<T>, where T is a class of
type PromotionData. In this case, it's our SpendAmountGetDiscount
promotion. At runtime, promotion engine will do a little magic
(read, refection), to get the processor which can process a
specific promotion - so it will look up for a class inherits from
PromotionProcessorBase<TSpecificPromotion> to process a
TSpecificPromotion promotion.
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                276

1 public class SpendAmountGetDiscountProcessor : PromotionP\

2 rocessorBase<SpendAmountGetDiscount>

3{

4     /// <summary>

5     /// Evaluates a promotion against an order form.

6     /// </summary>

7     public override RewardDescription Evaluate(SpendAmoun\

8 tGetDiscount promotionData, PromotionProcessorContext con\

9 text)

10    {

11       throw new NotImplementedException();

12    }

13 }

                  The processors can be reused. That means, if you have
                  a class SpendAmountGetDiscountEx which adds some
                  extra properties for data (and are not used in calcu-
                  lation), you don't have to create a processor for it -
                  you can just reuse SpendAmountGetDiscountProcessor
                 - PromotionEngine can handle it just fine.

    There is a method we have to implement - Evaluate will
    take a PromotionData which contains the conditions and
    rewards of the promotion, and the a context (where we can
    get information about other data such as the OrderGroup itself).
    This PromotionProcessorContext contains almost any information
    from the "context" we need to evaluate the promotion:
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                277

1 public class PromotionProcessorContext

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the <see cref="IOrderGroup"/> that is being \

5 evaluated.

6     /// </summary>

7     public IOrderGroup OrderGroup { get; }

8

9     /// <summary>

10    /// Gets the current order form that the promotion en\

11 gine is processing at the time.

12    /// </summary>

13    public IOrderForm OrderForm { get; }

14

15    /// <summary>

16    /// Gets the current order form sub total that the pr\

17 omotion engine is processing at the time. This is calcula\

18 ted before every promotion has been evaluated.

19    /// </summary>

20    public decimal CurrentOrderFormSubTotal { get; }

21 }

    For our processor, we will create a helper method to calculate the
    order subtotal - where we also need to care about the discounts
    applied by previous promotions.

1     private decimal GetOrderSubTotal(IOrderForm orderForm)

2     {

3         var lineItems = orderForm.Shipments.SelectMany(s \

4 => s.LineItems);

5         return lineItems.Sum(i => (i.PlacedPrice * i.Quan\

6 tity - i.LineItemDiscountAmount - i.OrderLevelDiscountAmo\

7 unt));

8     }

    And then Evaluate is simple enough. RewardDescription provides
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                             278

    a static method to create an instance with percentage discount:

1 public override RewardDescription Evaluate(SpendAmountGet\

2 Discount promotionData, PromotionProcessorContext context\

3)

4{

5      var orderForm = context.OrderForm;

6      var threshold = promotionData.Amounts.FirstOrDefault(\

7 c => c.Currency == context.OrderGroup.Currency);

8      var subTotal = GetOrderSubTotal(orderForm);

9      if (threshold.Amount <= subTotal)

10     {

11        var redemption = new List<RedemptionDescription> \

12 { new RedemptionDescription(new AffectedOrder(orderForm))\

13 };

14        return RewardDescription.CreatePercentageReward(F\

15 ulfillmentStatus.Fulfilled, redemption, promotionData, pr\

16 omotionData.Discount, "The promotion has been fulfilled")\

17 ;

18     }

19     else

20     {

21        return RewardDescription.CreateNotFulfilledDescri\

22 ption(promotionData, FulfillmentStatus.NotFulfilled);

23     }

24 }

    If the SubTotal of the orderform is greater than or equal the
    required amount, then we give the discount. As this will be affected
    the entire order, so we create AffectedItem(s) for all the entries.
    AffectedItem is the unit to let the PromotionEngine know which
    line items it should apply promotions to. All the information is
    stored in a Redemption, which is counted toward the redemption
    limits (if it is set). In case the amount does not satisfy the require-
    ment, we simply return a NotFulfilled RewardDescription.
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  279

Let's try to see if it works - Add a promotion with the required spent
amount of USD1500 and the discount amount of 10%, then try to
add something to the cart, of course, with the "WorkflowsVNext"
flag enabled.

                                        Ta da! It works

One interesting thing in above code is the RedemptionDescription
- the processor is supposed to return that "description" to the
promotion engine about the affected entities, the saved amount etc.
In this case, we have a promotion which affect the entire order, so
we return an AffectedOrder object which points to the orderForm.
In later examples, we will talk about other types of promotion
which takes lineitem or shipment, and they will have different
affected entities.

We kept it simple for demonstration, but let's consider the more ad-
vanced scenario: If you set the target to be $1500, and the customer
already has $1200 of items in cart, it's really nice to display some
message like "Spend $300 more and get a 10% discount". In the end,
we need to boost our sales while still make our customers happy, a
little push like this can be more effective than you would think (I
can speak for myself in this matter!)

To do that, let's add a property to SpendAmountGetDiscount promo-
tion (and of course you might want to add a localized string for this
new property):
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system             280

1 public virtual decimal AlmostFulfilledThreshold { get; se\
2 t; }

    This property allows editors to set the threshold that customers
    start seeing the notification to "spend more". Now we can update
    Evaluate method to handle this number:

1 if (threshold.Amount * AlmostFulfilledThreshold > subTota\

2 l)

3{

4     return RewardDescription.CreateNotFulfilledDescriptio\

5 n(promotionData, FulfillmentStatus.NotFulfilled);

6}

7 else

8{

9     var redemption = new List<RedemptionDescription> { ne\

10 w RedemptionDescription(new AffectedOrder(orderForm)) };

11    if (threshold.Amount < subTotal )

12    {

13       return RewardDescription.CreatePercentageReward(F\

14 ulfillmentStatus.Fulfilled, redemption, promotionData, pr\

15 omotionData.Discount, "The promotion has been fulfilled")\

16 ;

17    }

18    else

19    {

20       return RewardDescription.CreatePercentageReward(F\

21 ulfillmentStatus.PartiallyFulfilled, redemption, promotio\

22 nData, promotionData.Discount, "The promotion is almost f\

23 ulfilled");

24    }

25 }

    You now might wonder about which statuses you can use for
    RewardRedemption, here's the full list of FulfillmentStatus:
    Chapter 11: The new promotion system                  281

1 [Flags]

2 public enum FulfillmentStatus

3{

4     NotFulfilled = 1 << 0,

5

6     PartiallyFulfilled = 1 << 1,

7

8     Fulfilled = 1 << 2,

9

10    CouponCodeRequired = 1 << 8,

11    Excluded = 1 << 9,

12    VisitorGroupRequired = 1 << 10,

13    RedemptionLimitReached = 1 << 11,

14

15    InvalidCoupon = NotFulfilled | CouponCodeRequired,

16    InvalidCombination = NotFulfilled | Excluded,

17    MissingVisitorGroup = NotFulfilled | VisitorGroupRequ\

18 ired,

19    NoRedemptionRemaining = NotFulfilled | RedemptionLimi\

20 tReached,

21 }

    Normally, you will only return one of first three statuses:
    NotFulfilled, PartiallyFulfilled or Fulfilled from your
    processor. Other statuses are expected to be returned by the
    implementation of IPromotionEngine. (Of course, nothing prevent
    you from implementing it your own and use those statuses as
    well).

    Now we created a promotion which can return a
    PartiallyFulfilled RewardDescription in case the customer
    is close to their reward. Of course, as the seller, you want to let
    them know about that - to encourage them to spend some more
    money to get rewarded. You should be able to check it when
    a customer adds a new item to the cart - a quick evaluation
    for promotions, but nothing is applied. That's when the other
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  282

overload of IPromotionEngine.Run comes in to play - you can pass
a PromotionEngineSettings which specifies the statuses you are
interested in, and if you want to apply the reward or not. Here's
the code in the "Mini Cart" can look like:

1 var rewards = _promotionEngine.Run(cart, new PromotionEng\
2 ineSettings() { RequestedStatuses = RequestFulfillmentSta\
3 tus.PartiallyFulfilled, ApplyReward = false });
4 var almostFulfilledPromotions = rewards.Where(r => r.Stat\
5 us == FulfillmentStatus.PartiallyFulfilled);

    You now have a list of almost fulfilled promotions - and it's up to
    you to display some encouragement to the customers. They will be
    happy spending more money and you will be happy selling more
    items. It's a win-win situation!

                         An EntryPromotion

Now we create our first promotion with the new promotion system,
I hope that you liked it - or at least found it easy enough. But
we should not stop there. As an expression which is common in
Sweden: "Practices make perfect" (I'm sure it's said everywhere,
but a little more here), we should try to create another one to make
sure we fully understand the system. Let's say we want to give a
10% discount on any items which have the prices between 20 and
30 USD.

                              Unit testing

As I mentioned earlier, one of the reasons for the existing of
new promotion system, is to make it easier to write tests. With
the old system, the only possible way for automation test is to
write integration tests: Set up a running environment mimicking
a working site, then try to create a campaign, a promotion (which
is, tedious) , then create a cart and add line items to it, and call the
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  283

workflows to get the promotion applied, and verify the result. Trust
me, you won't like it.

The approach in new promotion system allows you to easily write
unit test for your processor. It is much more easier to mock
your class up and verify your logic only. While you should write
integration tests nonetheless, you should prefer to write unit tests -
they are easier to understand, and fast to run. You can use any unit
testing framework of choice, but in this example we will use xUnit,
one of the most popular options (which is also used by Episerver
Commerce team internally).

                Promotions exclusion

                       What you can exclude

The promotion system provides a very powerful exclusion mecha-
nism, to allow you quickly set the exclusion combination between
promotions.

Programmatically, the exclusion settings for a promotion can be
set by ExcludedItems, which is a collection of ContentReferences
you want to exclude, or be excluded by your promotion. - If it is
CampaignRoot, then the promotion will exclude all other promo-
tions. - If it is a SalesCampaign, then the promotion will exclude
all promotions in that campaign. - If it is a specific PromotionData,
that specific promotion will be excluded.

The exclusion setting comes in pair with priority, promotions
higher priority will exclude the promotions with lower priorities,
not the way around.
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  284

                       You can exclude anything and everything

              ExcludedItems is an example of bad property nam-
              ing (ambiguity). A lot of developers had confusion
              that this is the property indicates catalog items
              that would be excluded by this promotion. That is
              ExcludedCatalogItems we will talk about later - but
              moral of the story: make your property as unambigu-
              ous as possible. It does not have to be short, but it has
              to be clear of what it does.

                          Type of exclusion

There are two types of exclusion: per order, or per unit. Per order
means that the two excluded promotions cannot be applied to same
Chapter 11: The new promotion system                  285

order, even though this order is eligible for both. The higher priority
order will be applied first and that's it. Per unit means that two
excluded promotions cannot be applied to same line items.

By default, the exclusion is per order. If you want to change the
exclusion is per unit, PromotionExclusionLevel setting must be
added to appSettings with value is "Unit".

In Commerce 14, the setting is Episerver__Commerce_-
_Promotion__PromotionExclusionLevel, note the dou-
ble underscores.

                         Items exclusion

                         Exclusion per promotion

    For each promotion, you might want specific catalog items to
    be excluded. For example you want to have a promotion for
    mobile phones, except iPhones, because of a deal with the distrib-
    utor. That's where ExcludedCatalogItems comes into play. It's a
    IList<ContentReference> just like ExcludedItems, but instead of
    promotional items, you can add catalog items to it - - If it is a
    ContentReference to a CatalogContent, all content below will be
    excluded - If it is a ContentReference to a NodeContent, all sub-
    categories and entries will be excluded - If it is a ContentReference
    to a EntryContentBase, that specific entry (or the SKUs, in case of
    products) will be excluded.

    And just like ExcludedItems, ExcludedCatalogItems can be any
    combination of those ContentReference:s.

1 p.ExcludedCatalogItems = new List<ContentReference>(new[]\
2 { CategoryLink });
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  286

as ExcludedCatalogItems is a property of PromotionData, to change
it you need to create a writeable clone first, and then after that, save
it.

                  Exclusion for all promotions

There are things that should never be discounted. For example -
gift cards. You might have a 10% off site wide, except gift cards
(otherwise your customers will keep buying gift cards using gift
cards and virtually get it for free). Or you have items from a luxury
brand that do not want to be discounted. Of course for each and
every promotion you can add exclusion to those items, but that
would be a very tedious, repetitive and boring task. Is there a better
way? Yes!

EntryFilterSettings is your new friend. It's a very flexible and
powerful way to exclude any entries from promotion engine.
The common way to do it is to add your filters in one of your
IInitializeModule. You can simply exclude a type:

1 entryFilterSettings.AddFilter<GiftCardContent>(x => false)

    the type can be any class or interface. You can also exclude any
    content with a certain property value

1 entryFilterSettings.AddFilter<EntryContentBase>(x => !(bo\
2 ol)(x["IsGiftCard"] ?? false));

    AddFilter takes a Predicate<T> so it can be very flexible - sky is
    your limit.

    Another major difference between EntryFilterSettings and
    ExcludedCatalogItems is that EntryFilterSettings is applied
    on the fly, and it's code-only, nothing is saved to database.
    ExcludedCatalogItems needs to be saved (along with the
    promotion) to take effect.
Chapter 11: The new promotion system  287

Lastly, it does not have a RemoveFilter method. If you want to
remove a filter, only have to clear it using ClearFilters, and start
again.
    Part 7: Business

       Foundation

This chapter is dedicated to one of the most advance and in some
ways, most mysterious features in Episerver Commerce - Business
Foundation. What is it, how does it work, how can it be extended.

Chapter 13: The basic

We have learned about the MetaData system - with the abilities to
extend the Catalog and Order system. However, they all have to
be extended on their "base" classes. For the business, sometimes
you need to work with entities which are not catalog, or order.
Then comes the Business Foundation, the most flexible, yet most
complicated system in entire Optimizely Commerce, where you can
define any entities as you like. Business Foundation is built around
a data-modeling system, and you can define any models from the
basic elements, it's essentially an ORM.

Previously Business Foundation was strictly in Commerce Manager.
With Commerce 14 when Commerce Manager dies, it was left as
an API-only part without any UI. Thankfully in later version of
Commerce 14, the UI to manage Business Foundation objects has
been added. Now you have a new, modern, intuitive way to CRUD
them:
                                                                                289

                   It is important to know that Business Foundation has a
                   limit of 40 columns per table. So if you class is very big
                   (it is not something you should do, but still), the extra
                   fields will be added to a _ex0, _ex1 and so on. There
                   is no guarantee that this field will be in this table, so
                   custom queries for Business Foundation classes can be
                   very tricky.

    Under the neath, you will always be using BusinessManager and
    its methods. BusinessManager is a static class, and almost all of its
    methods will be working on EntityObject.

1 BusinessManager.Create(entityObject);

                   The tidbit here is all public methods of
                   BusinessManager actually call to Execute(Request
                   request), where the Request will contain information
                   about what action to perform. So as you can see, this
                   goes deep.

    It is an important thing to know that by default, when you
    use BusinessManager.List, it will only returns the first 1000
    objects. You can, in theory, change the number of objects by
    MetaObjectOptions.MaxListObject setting, but it has its own
    drawbacks (you generally want to avoid loading too much in
    one go). This is due to the fact that many parts of the Business
    Foundation do not use paging (and until later versions, there is
    no actual paging API). Nothing will change in the near future,
    unfortunately.

                              Contacts

    Contact is probably the most commonly used Business Foundation
    objects. So common, in fact, it has "strongly" typed class defined
                                                                              290

   for it, and specifically defined features for it. As you might guess,
   contact is the information associated with one of the user. It does
   not contains information such as user name or password, but
   almost any other information: first name, last name, addresses etc.

                 Contact actually has a field for password, but it was
                 there for historical reasons. The Contact itself should
                 not be used for authentication, that belongs to the
                 identity system (AspNetIdentity, or if you have a very
                 old site, Membership provider).

1 public class CustomerContact : ContactEntity

   CustomerContact is an extension of ContactEntity, which itself is
   an extension of EntityObject. You will be managing contacts by
   using CustomerContext, however.

   There is a caveat here, while CustomerContext provides plenty of
   utility methods, it does not have one that get exactly one contact
   based on a specific criteria - most importantly, by email address.

   This is how you does it

1 try

2{

3      var filterEl = new FilterElement("Email", FilterEleme\

4 ntType.Equal, email);

5      return

6      BusinessManager.List(ContactEntity.ClassName, new\

7 [] { filterEl })

8              .OfType<CustomerContact>()

9              .FirstOrDefault();

10 }

11 catch (ObjectNotFoundException)

12 {
                    291

13    //Safe guard
14    return null;
15 }

This filter pattern can be used for almost anything - not limited to
email address, and not limited to contacts. However, it comes with
a caution: filtering by a specific field means a query on that column.
If you are going to call this a lot, make sure you have proper indexes.
We will discuss in detail in later section.

                      Organizations

            Performance considerations

While my general advice is to not using Business Foundation
outside of provided features (like contacts and organizations man-
agement), you will from time to time need to BF to implement the
feature you need.

It's critical to have correct indexes to your tables. One example that
I usually give is that in Sweden there is a social security number,
"personnummer", which literally translate to "person number". It is
used to identify in many websites and services. So now, if you are
building a website in Sweden and you allow your customers to store
their personnummer there (mind you, it is not as "sensitive" as the
SSN in the US, you don't print yours on a business card and give
to people, but it is not a super secret), and then you want to get the
customer based on their personnummer, it makes a very good sense
to add a non clustered index on that column in your contact table.
Why? Because it's unique, it has a very good value distribution.
Part 8: Extending the
          system.

Optimizely Commerce Cloud is not just a software. It IS a frame-
work. Out of the box, it should work with basic scenarios, but
you will need something specialized for your implementation. You
might want to talk with a CRM system, or to use a PIM to update
catalog, or to get your ERP to handle the orders for you. Good news:
Optimizely Commerce Cloud allows (or even, encourages) you to
do that. Most of the important parts of the system can be replaced
by your implementations. Then how? Let's discover it.
            Plug your

  implementations - The

      Framework way.

There are many ways to plug your implementations into Opti-
mizely Commerce Cloud. Mediachase eCF has its ways, but we'll be
talking about the method provided by Episerver Framework, first.

There are two initialization modules from Commerce you
might want to care about - the CommerceInitialization from
Mediachase.Commerce.Initialization, and InitializationModule,
from EPiServer.Commerce.Initialization. The latter depends on
the former (so it's guaranteed to run after). Why should you care?
CommerceInitialization will handle almost every dependencies
with core stuffs, such as ICatalogSystem, IInventoryService,
IMarketService, etc. InitializationModule will handle the
stuffs related to catalog content and routing. You will know
which module to depend on (of course if you depend on
InitializationModule, you will automatically depend on
CommerceInitialization as well).

However, their names do not tell the entire truth, in facts, they
inherit from IConfigurableModule, which itself inherits from

IInitializationModule

Here's how you can implement your IConfigurableModule:
    Plug your implementations - The Framework way.         294

1 [ModuleDependency(typeof(EPiServer.Commerce.Initializatio\

2 n.InitializationModule))]

3 public class InitializationModule : IConfigurableModule

4{

5     public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

6     {

7        //Do your stuffs here, such as register for event\

8 s.

9     }

10

11    public void Uninitialize(InitializationEngine context)

12    {

13       //Undo your stuffs, such as unregister the events.

14    }

15

16    public void ConfigureContainer(ServiceConfigurationCo\

17 ntext context)

18    {

19       //Configure the dependencies

20       context.Services.AddSingleton<ICurrentMarket, Mar\

21 ketStorage>();

22    }

23 }

    In the example above, we add MarketStorage as the singleton
    instance for ICurrentMarket, that means if you call this in your
    code:

1 var currentMarketService = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInst\
2 ance<ICurrentMarket>();

    You'll get the same instance of MarketStorage
    every time. AddSingleton is just one of 5 ways
    to register the life-cycle of your dependencies -
    ServiceConfigurationProviderExtensions provides plenty
Plug your implementations - The Framework way.  295

extensions methods for IServiceConfigurationProvider, but in
short you can choose between:

    · AddSingleton<TService, TImplementation>, as above
    · AddTransient<TService, TImplementation>: new instance of

      TImplementation will be created every time TService is re-
      quested.
    · AddScoped<TService, TImplementation>: registers the depen-
      dency to be "hybrid" - If HttpContext is available, then the
      life-cycle of TImplementation is per-HttpContext, otherwise
      it's per-thread when TService is requested.
    · AddHttpContextScoped<TService, TImplementation>: new
      instance of TImplementation will be created for every
      HttpRequest.
    · AddAddHttpContextOrThreadScoped: this should be equiva-
      lent with AddScoped.

Remember to choose the life-cycle widely, especially when you
class has states stored. Having wrong life-cycle can messed up with
states and can cause nasty problems, especially in concurrent cases.

The second way to register dependencies is to use
ServiceConfigurationAttribute, for example:

1 [ServiceConfiguration(typeof(ApplicationContext), Lifecyc\
2 le = ServiceInstanceScope.Singleton)]
3 [ServiceConfiguration(typeof(IApplicationContext), Factor\
4 yMember = "Instance")] // instance (otherwise the contain\
5 er will keep one singleton for each interface/type)
6 public class ApplicationContext : IApplicationContext
7{
8}

    Here the list of scope you can choose from (There are other scopes,
    such as HybridHttpSession, however they will be obsolete soon):
    Plug your implementations - The Framework way.     296

1 public enum ServiceInstanceScope

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// New instance per usage. Equivalent to AddTransien\

5 t.

6     /// </summary>

7     Transient,

8

9     /// <summary>

10    /// One instance per container. Equivalent to AddSing\

11 leton.

12    /// </summary>

13    Singleton,

14

15    /// <summary>

16    /// New instance per request. Equivalent to Add

17    /// </summary>

18    HttpContext,

19

20    /// <summary>

21    /// New instance per thread or request. Equivalent to\

22 AddScoped.

23    /// </summary>

24    Hybrid,

25 }

    Normally, you should use ServiceConfigurationAttribute to reg-
    ister your dependencies, it's more convenient way. However, there
    are cases when you need to use IConfigurableModule instead: to
    control the order of dependencies registration. If you want to
    override a certain implementation of a system interface, and you
    know it is registered in CommerceInitialization, for example -
    you can make your IConfigurableModule depends on that module,
    therefore your dependency registration will be guaranteed to run
    after the system one. In the world of DI, the later registration wins.
Plug your implementations - The Framework way.  297

There are two notable IConfigurableModule you should know
about Episerver Commerce:

    · CommerceInitialization in Mediachase.Commerce names-
      pace. This can be considered as the "main" module where
      almost important stuffs from Commerce are registered.

    · InitializationModule in EPiServer.Commerce.Initialization.
      This is where content and routing stuffs are registered.

Unless you have strong reasons to have dependency on
CommerceInitialization, it's usually safer to make your
IConfigurableModule depends on InitializationModule to
make sure every Commerce registrations have been done.
It's is important to note that there is no guarantee about the
order of execution for two modules which have dependency
on same other module. So if your module depends on
CommerceInitialization, it's undetermined that it will run
before or after InitializationModule.

OK that's enough of theoretical information. Let's get our hands
dirty with some code.

ICurrentMarket is a small interface with only two methods: to get
and to set the current market. The class MarketStorage in Enoteca
and Commerce Sample site simply allows you set current market
(selected from a dropdown) to store in cookie, and load it back. In
real world, you might not want customers to do that, the market
must be automatically assigned from customers GeoIP (customers
might be able to select the market afterward, but the market is
assigned to them for the first time they visit)
        The unusual ways.

   As mentioned in previous chapters, Mediachase eCF has its own
   ways to extend the framework. It's not as unified or easy to
   understand as the way Episerver Framework provides, but it still
   does its job when you needs it. We will discuss case by case to see
   how you can extend the framework in each way.

       Customize order processing

   Until the new order system is in place (And you are willing to
   make the move), it might seem impossible to customize how orders
   (including cart, payment plan and purchase order) are handled. Not
   quite. In facts, it's quite easy to do so, with the support from the
   order configuration system.

   Let's assume you want to do extra thing whenever you save a
   purchase order. You might want to call an external API:s to update
   some information, for example. How?

   Let's first create your custom class:

1 public class MyPurchaseOrder : PurchaseOrder

2{

3   public override void AcceptChanges()

4   {

5      //Your fancy code goes here.

6      base.AcceptChanges();

7   }

8}

   You can even remove base.AcceptChanges(). That means you don't
   want to save the order into database in normal way. You might save
The unusual ways.  299

it to your custom persistent backend, a document database might be.
Note that if you goes that way, the static methods in PurchaseOrder
class to search and load for orders, such as LoadByCustomer(Guid
CustomerId) will no longer works and some features depend on
it, like the OrderManagement in Commerce Manager, will break
(unless you go even further and extend them as well). You're on
your very own now.

And now the magic parts: To let the system know that you want
to use MyPurchaseOrder instead of PurchaseOrder. Just go to
Configs\ecf.order.config, and update the line in MappedTypes/Pur-
chaseOrderType with your new type, note that it must be the full
name of the type:

1 <PurchaseOrderType name="MySite.Order.Classes.MyPurchaseO\
2 rder, MySite" />

    Now build it and you're done. Try to place an order and see the
    magic happens.

    The above step applies for front-end site. If you want to use it in
    Commerce Manager as well, make sure to deploy your assembly to
    its bin folder, and update the its config file as well.

    This method also works with other metaclasses, such as Cart or
    PaymentPlan, and even OrderForm and LineItem. In the end, you
    have the power all over the order system!

         Work with "workflows".

No I lied, at least partly. It's not the workflows anymore.
Episerver Commerce has a special, open-sourced project
(Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow) ¹, which was based on
Windows Workflow Foundation (WF) 3.5 in Commerce 8 and

    ¹Can be downloaded from here
The unusual ways.  300

earlier. In Commerce9, the engine has been rewritten to remove
the dependencies on WF, and the "workflow" has now been
renamed to ActivityFlow, to indicate that it now longer depends
on WF. Still, the term workflow is more familiar and we will use
that term in this book.

The reason Episerver Commerce open-sources Medi-
achase.Commerce.Workflow is to allow you to customize the
workflows to fit with your business requirements. The choice is
yours - either attach the project into your solution and modify
it directly, or inject your "activity" into the existing workflows
replace the default one.¹

               How workflows are registered

     If you worked with Windows Workflow Foundation before, you'll
     probably know about XOML (Extensible Object Markup Language)
     files. They were used to define the workflows in Commerce 8 and
     prior, and here's how an XOML would look like

 1 <SequentialWorkflowActivity x:Class="Mediachase.Commerce.\
 2 Workflow.CartCheckoutWorkflow" x:Name="CartCheckoutWorkfl\
 3 ow" xmlns:ns0="clr-namespace:Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow\
 4 .Activities" xmlns:ns1="clr-namespace:Mediachase.Commerce\
 5 .Workflow.Activities.Cart" xmlns:ns2="clr-namespace:Media\
 6 chase.Commerce.Workflow.Activities.PurchaseOrderActivitie\
 7 s" xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml"\
 8 xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/work\
 9 flow">
10 <IfElseActivity x:Name="ifElseActivity1">

           ¹It was a known limitation with Commerce 8 and prior that if you reference to the

        workflow assembly, you will have to stick with EPiServer.Framework package which your
        EPiServer.Commerce.Core package was built with . This was caused by the build engine of
        WF as it skips the assembly binding configured in web.config. Such limitation was removed in
        Commerce 9. One more reason to upgrade your solution to the latest Commerce version and
        stay on top of your game?
    The unusual ways.                                      301

11  <IfElseBranchActivity x:Name="ifElseBranchActivity1">

12                     <IfElseBranchActivity.Condition>

13                     <CodeCondition Condition="RunProcessPayment" />

14                     </IfElseBranchActivity.Condition>

15                     <ns1:ProcessPaymentActivity Warnings="{x:Null}" OrderGr\

16 oup="{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}"\

17 Payment="{x:Null}" x:Name="processPaymentActivity1" />

18  </IfElseBranchActivity>

19 </IfElseActivity>

20 <ns0:CalculateTotalsActivity x:Name="calculateTotalsActiv\

21 ity1" OrderGroup="{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path\

22 =OrderGroup}" Warnings="{x:Null}" />

23 <ns1:AdjustInventoryActivity Description="Adjusts Invento\

24 ry." x:Name="adjustInventoryActivity1" OrderGroup="{Activ\

25 ityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}" Warnings="\

26 {ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=Warnings}" />

27 <ns1:RecordPromotionUsageActivity UsageStatus="Used" Warn\

28 ings="{x:Null}" OrderGroup="{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWor\

29 kflow,Path=OrderGroup}" x:Name="recordPromotionUsageActiv\

30 ity1" />

31 </SequentialWorkflowActivity>

    You might guess - this is the CartCheckout workflow, and it
    has a quite simple logic. If the condition RunProcessPayment is
    true, then run ProcessPaymentActivity. Then run CalculateTotal-
    sActivity and AdjustInventoryActivity. And finally RecordPromo-
    tionUsageActivity.

    Not too complicated, right? But there are several major drawbacks:
    - It's overly complicated for a simple logic. If it was written in C#, it
    would be on 4-5 lines of code. - It's hard to know an activity belongs
    to which workflows. You can only search, instead of using the Go
    to implementation or Find all references provided by Visual Studio.
    - It's hard to edit and even worse, hard to debug. The workflows
    had always puzzled me when it came to breakpoints and so.

    Luckily for us (myself included), Commerce 9 removed the depen-
    The unusual ways.                                  302

    dencies of Workflow Foundation, and moved to a new, almost
    "POCO" (Plain Old C# Object) approach. Here's how the above
    activity looks like now:

1 using Mediachase.Commerce.Engine;

2 using Mediachase.Commerce.Orders.Managers;

    Tell me how long did it take you to understand what it intends to
   The unusual ways.                                          303

   do? I even included the using statements and the doc comments
   and it's still shorter than the XOML version. I can now see where
   an activity is used easily by features provided by Visual Studio, and
   debug without sweating and grinning. Life just got easier!

   OK, so let's break down how the new "workflows" are registered.

   At first, it's supposed to be inherited from ActivityFlow (as we
   mentioned earlier, it's the way Episerver used to distinct this from
   Workflow term - so we know it's the new engine). By convention,
   the class will have the suffix -ActivityFlow, but you are free to
   choose otherwise.

   The class should be decorated with an ActivityFlowConfiguration
   attribute. It has two important properties: - Name, which is manda-
   tory. In this example, the name of the workflow is "CartCheckout",
   which is supposed to be a system workflow. - Priority, which is
   optional. In case there are multiple workflows with a same name,
   the workflow with highest priority will take effect. By default,
   priority = 0, then any priority higher than take will take effect.

   So if you want to have your own CartCheckout but don't want to
   mess with the default one, it's simple as this:

1  /// <summary>

2  /// This class represents the Cart Checkout workflow

3  /// </summary>

4  [ActivityFlowConfiguration(Name = OrderGroupWorkflowM\

5 anager.CartCheckOutWorkflowName, Priority=10)]

6  public class MyCartCheckoutActivityFlow : ActivityFlow

7  {

8  }

   And finally, a method to configure how the workflow acts:
The unusual ways.  304

1 public override ActivityFlowRunner Configure(ActivityFlow\

2 Runner activityFlow)

3{

4}

    You can use a fluent API:s on activityFlow to configure the condi-
    tion an action, with three basic elements: If(Func<bool>), EndIf()
    and Do<T>(). If will be the conditional branching, while Do<T> will
    execute an activity.

    There is another attribute for ActivityFlowConfiguration you
    might need to know about - AvailableInBetaMode. Only work-
    flows decorated with this attribute are available in BETA mode
    (VNextFeatures enabled). However, at the time this book completes,
    I would expect this attribute to be removed (along with all BETA
    tags in the API:s)

    If you have customer workflows from Commerce 8, make sure to
    check out the workflow migration tool¹. It's pretty cool and will
    convert your workflow project into new format in (almost) just one
    click!

    Let's go through an example on how you can customize the
    workflow project. Normally, when a customer adds items to the
    cart, the system will have to check the inventory to see if there are
    enough goods in the store, and then when he or she actually places
    an order, the system will have to adjust the quantities. However,
    this is not necessary if your website only sells downloadable games.
    You can remove those steps altogether for simpler processing.

    There are three "Activity" which relate to such actions:

        · GetFulfillmentWarehouseActivity to get the warehouse
           which can fulfill the order.

        · CheckInstoreInventoryActivity or CheckInventoryActivity
           to check if the inventory can fulfill the order.

         ¹http://world.episerver.com/download/Items/EPiServer-Commerce/episerver-commerce-

      9-tools-and-code-samples/
    The unusual ways.                 305

        · AdjustInventoryActivity to actually adjust the inventory
           according to purchasing number.

    Also, ActivityFlow has a property named ShouldAdjustInventory,
    to indicate if the activity flow should adjust the inventory. Because
    in this case it's always false, to we will remove it as well.

    We can start by removing above activities form the project. Because
    some ActivityFlows use them, they need to be updated as well. For

           ProfileModule

     Several features of Episerver frameworks in general and Episerver
     Commerce in particular - are provided by IHttpModule implementa-
     tions. When you need to register an action as soon as the site starts
     - this is probably the best place to be. Here are the default modules
     in a standard Commerce installation:

 1 <modules runAllManagedModulesForAllRequests="true">
 2 <add name="BusinessFoundationInitializeModule" preCondi\
 3 tion="managedHandler" type="Mediachase.Commerce.Core.Modu\
 4 les.BusinessFoundationInitializeModule, Mediachase.Commer\
 5 ce" />
 6 <add name="InitializationModule" type="EPiServer.Framew\
 7 ork.Initialization.InitializationModule, EPiServer.Framew\
 8 ork" preCondition="managedHandler" />
 9 <add name="UrlRewriteModule" type="EPiServer.Web.Routin\
10 gUrlRewriteModule, EPiServer" preCondition="managedHandle\
11 r" />
12 <add name="ShellRoutingModule" type="EPiServer.Shell.We\
13 b.Routing.ShellRoutingModule, EPiServer.Shell" />
14 <remove name="WebDAVModule" />
15 <add name="ProfileModule" type="EPiServer.Business.Comm\
16 erce.HttpModules.ProfileModule, EPiServer.Business.Commer\
17 ce" />
18 <add name="CatalogNodeModule" type="EPiServer.Business.\
19 Commerce.HttpModules.CatalogNodeModule, EPiServer.Busines\
20 s.Commerce" />
21 </modules>
    ProfileModule                                              308

          You can ignore CatalogNodeModule because it's ob-
          soleted and does not really do anything (future re-
          lease of Commerce will get rid of that). And you
          should not touch other modules because they pro-
          vide critical functions in Episerver Framework and
          Commerce. The (only) one we can change here is the
          ProfileModule.

    So what we can do with ProfileModule? ProfileModule was reg-
    istered so it kicks in when a customer logs in. It will try to assign
    the last order (placed when anonymous) to the current user profile,
    and merge carts (if not empty) to the existing carts (there might be
    multiple carts in a multi markets set up, and wishlist is also counted
    as one), if the carts are not empty. However, that might not be the
    behaviors we want, and we're going to replace that.

    First, we need to implement IHttpModule. IHttpModule is just a
    simple ASP.NET interface:

1 public interface IHttpModule

2{

3     // Summary:

4     //           Disposes of the resources (other than memory) \

5 used by the module that implements

6     //           System.Web.IHttpModule.

7     void Dispose();

8

9     // Summary:

10    //           Initializes a module and prepares it to handle\

11 requests.

12    void Init(HttpApplication context);

13 }

    After implementing your class, simply replace the registration of
    ProfileModule in <modules> section with your type - the type value
    should be the full name of your type.
    The Migration Steps

Migration Step is one of the hidden features of Episerver Commerce.
It's been there since Episerver Commerce 7.10 (I remember it better
than anything else because it can be considered my baby). It's not
public, it's even in a separated assembly with "Internal" part in its
name (EPiserver.Commerce.Internal.Migration). And if you browse
its API:s, there will be document clearly states that these API:s
should only be used for Episerver infrastructure.

Then should you use it?

Well, the answer, as always, is: it depends. A Migration Step is
supposed be one-time only operation. The initialization modules
provide one-time operation per every start, but here, it's one per
website. Episerver has been using Migration steps as the data-
modifications for updates (it's not necessary to have one migration
step per update, in facts, the chance you run into migration steps
for a minor update is relatively small) . They have the upgrade
SQL files to do DDL operations, such as update stored procedures,
add/remove columns, etc, and Migration steps for migrating data,
fixing something up. It's not strictly separated, thought. Sometimes
upgrade SQL files can be used to migrate data if it's easier and faster
that way, but you can see where each one should fit.

The idea of Migration Step is quite simple. You implement a special
interface (Can you guess it name? Well, IMigrationStep), which has
one method named Execute. The method will return true or false
to indicate its result. Every time your site start, MigrationManager
will check if there is any steps which are not run, and depends on
your setting, it might redirect you to a special page to run remaining
steps, or start them automatically. So, migration steps are run
after initialization modules, and before anything else. There's a
mechanism to make sure you have to complete migration steps
    The Migration Steps                                       310

    (for safety reasons, of course. You won't want to run your site
    with incorrect data). When a step returns true to indicate it ran
    successfully, MigrationManager will store the information in the
    database to remember for the next time. Otherwise, it does mean
    some errors happened, you might need to correct them before
    trying again. When all migration steps are done, you can return
    to normal operations.

                                        Migration steps view

    OK let's try to implement a Migration Step.

1 public interface IMigrationStep

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Gets the order of the step. The one with lowest o\

5 rder executes first.

6   /// </summary>

7   int Order { get; }

8

9   /// <summary>

10  /// Gets the name of the step

11  /// </summary>

12  string Name { get; }

13

14  /// <summary>
   The Migration Steps                                    311

15    /// Executes the step.
16    /// </summary>
17    bool Execute(IProgressMessenger processMessenger);
18
19    /// <summary>
20    /// Gets or sets the description.
21    /// </summary>
22    string Description { get; }
23 }

   The two things you need to care about is Order and Execute. Order
   defines which migration step to run first - the lower Order, the
   higher it is in the queue (as you might expect, the lowest orders are
   reserved for the old built in migration steps). Execute simply runs
   the code, it takes an IProcessManager to let you display the progress
   in Migration view.

   Migration settings:

   You can configure the Migration Steps to automatically run when
   the site starts (and if there are steps which haven't run successfully,
   of course). You can set it either by setting (Add a key named
   AutoMigrateEPiServer to your appSettings section, with value as
   true), or by code, in your IInitializationModule:

1 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

2{

3     context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<MigrationSettings\

4 >().AutoMigrate = true;

5}

   If the setting by configuration is present, it will always override the
   one by code.

   The migration steps reside in EPiServer.Commerce.Core package,
   but the migration view resides in EPiServer.Commerce.UI
   one, which make senses because you always need to run
The Migration Steps  312

the steps, but you only want to see the steps if you have
access to editor UI. However, this can be an issue if you have
installation without EPiServer.Commerce.UI (you might only
install EPiServer.Commerce.UI on the content authoring instance,
but not instances serving visitors). If you configured the migration
steps to automatically run, then it will start without any problem,
but if you didn't, an exception will be thrown during site
startup. To avoid that issue, always start the installation with
EPiServer.Commerce.UI first.

The migration steps cannot run in the context of Commerce Man-
ager. However, there is a safe check which will throw an exception
if you start your Commerce Manager website without having the
migration steps completed.

                              It is not scary as it seems to be

In such case, simply run the migration steps in the front-end site,
then restart Commerce Manager (a simple refresh won't do).
   Part 9: Advanced
           stuffs

Finally, some advanced stuffs
       Caching

There are only two hard things in Computer Science:
cache invalidation and naming things.

­ Phil Karlton

My former boss liked this quote so much that he usually quoted
it when we have a discussion about naming in our pull request
reviews. But of course, cache invalidation is even harder and it's
critical to your code correctness.

For a high performance site, you will sooner or later have to use
cache (Fact: the sooner, the better). Getting cache right is hard - it's
an art of balancing between performance, memory and ease (Of
course, correctness is a non-compromise requirement).

There are very little few guidelines for caching in a Optimizely
Commerce site, it depends a lot in your implementation. However,
those following best practices are something you should really
follow:

· Use  ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache             instead

of IObjectInstanceCache. This interface comes

from Optimizely Framework and it's basically the

backbone of caching system in entire Commerce. The

major difference with IObjectInstanceCache is that

ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache works for multiple

instances scenarios - which is almost a given today. It's a

quite simple interface with some methods to play with.

       Some bugs were introduced because
       IObjectInstanceCache was used where
       ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache should be.
Caching  315

One thing to keep in mind is that ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache,
at least the default implementation, only guarantees that the cache
validation is synchronized. So if you call Remove(key) in your
class, you're guaranteed that other instances of your site will not
read the obsolete cache value anymore. It does not guarantee that
the cache is present in all instances, they will still be loaded from
database or network, if nothing is present in cache.

    · Think about the cache key. Keep it short (while still being
      unique). The cache engine will have a match a lot of keys and
      long keys will give more pressures on your CPUs.

    · Don't cache too much. Optimizely Commerce already caches
      (almost) everything it needs, from contents to carts. You
      should only cache your own stuffs when you really know
      it'll hit the database if you don't. Having too many objects
      floating around in cache is a bad thing - it will consume mem-
      ory (to some points, your system will throw OutOfMemory
      exception, or even worse, start saving RAM to page file), and
      having too many keys will slowdown the cache validation
      (key matching should still be very fast, but the master keys
      and dependent keys which is the issue).

You might ask what are actually cached in the system:

    · Catalog content are cached by ContentProvider,
      except when a content it was loaded with
      ContentReference.GetPublishedOrLatest is true. This
      cache is managed by CMS system.

    · DTO:s are cached by default.
    · Carts are cached by default.
    · IPriceValue:s are cached by default.
    · Think carefully about the dependent keys, master keys, and

      the caching mode. It's best when you can fire-and-forget.
      Having an obsolete cache item might harm your code correct-
      ness, and in some situations it's even worse than no cache at
Caching  316

  all. Internally, Optimizely applies a pattern like this, and it
  works quite well:

     ­ Define a cache key prefix which is short and unique,
        following the defined convention, for example:
        EP:EC:Catalog, where Catalog is the area we want to
        have cache.

     ­ Define a master key from the cache key:
        EP:EC:Catalog:*.

     ­ Define a cache key by the prefix + unique id, for exam-
        ple, EP:EC:Catalog:1, and when add the cache, add the
        master key to the list as well.

     ­ To clear the cache structure, simple call

          ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache.Remove(masterKey)

· It's hugely important to use the right overload. If you add
  a master key, and that key does not exist in cache, the
  Framework will add an empty object for that key for you. But
  if you add a dependency key and that key does not exist in
  cache, your cache object will be invalidated immediately (i.e.
  not adding to cache at all). There are several overloads which
  take master keys and dependent keys, and it's quite easy to
  misuse. Your code will still compile, and run without issues,
  only with performance hit which can be difficult to figure out.
  And don't feel too bad if one day you realized you are using
  the wrong overload and your code is, in fact, not using cache.
  I've been there too.
             Routing

Routing is important for any sites, and it's even more important for
a Commerce site - it can help driven sales. People nowadays search
for everything, and ranking higher in the search results yield much
better chance of your products being purchased.
There are two routing systems in Optimizely Commerce
(as always!). The old one is based on SEO URI, has
been there since Mediachase days, and the newer one,
HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter, which is a partial router,
was introduced in Commerce 7.5.

           The SEO Uri approach

This approach is based on the SEO Uri, so the link to your product
will be in form of http://<yourdomain>/your-product.aspx. Note
that .aspx is the default, but you are free to choose another exten-
sion, or even no extension at all. The SEO Uri can be edited in both
Commerce Manager and Catalog UI
Routing  318

                        SEO Uri editing in Commerce Manager

Those information are stored in CatalogItemSeo for both Nodes and
Entries, which each row represents the information of one node or
one entry in one language. Note that only the Uri is unique and is
used by the system, it's up to your site implementation to utilize
other information such as Title, Description - just follow best SEO
practices.

              If you are familiar with Optimizely Content Cloud,
              the SEO Uri is somewhat equivalent to simple address
              for CMS content.

                    The SEO Uri regeneration

The Uri is unique per language, and if you don't set it explicitly,
the catalog system will automatically generate them based on the
entry/node's name while still ensure the uniqueness. The default
implementation will work like this:

    · If the SEO Uri is empty, generate it from the name by
      replacing any invalid characters, possibly add the language
      code, and append the .aspx extension.
   Routing                                          319

       · If the Uri being saved is unique, save it
       · If it is not unique, generate again by adding a random string

         to the end. (it's the 8 first characters of a newly created GUID)

   This behavior can be overridden by inheriting from
   Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog.Data.UniqueSeoGenerator, and
   then register your implementation to override the default one.

   UniqueSeoGenerator is a simple class and all of its methods and
   properties are virtual, to override the Uri generation, you can
   override three following:

1 public class UniqueSeoGenerator

2{

3   protected virtual string UriExtension { get; }

4

5   public virtual string GenerateSeoUri(string name, str\

6 ing languageCode, bool includeRandomToken)

7

8   protected virtual string GetRandomToken()

9}

   Usually, you will have the Uri set by some other external systems, or
   manually, but there are still cases when the Uri:s are automatically
   generated. There are several tips to make the Uri generation more
   effective:

    · Change the default UriExtension. It was kept ".aspx" for
      the backward compatibility, but I don't think that kind of
      extension is popular anymore.

    · Make sure your entries, especially your variations, to have
      unique names. This will generate better Seo URIs - no matter
      how you override GetRandomToken - having some artificial
      random string append to your Uri can't be a good thing.
Routing                                                           320

    · When you made your Uri public, stick with it. Nothing worse
      than a hoping customer sees an 404 error for a product they
      planned to buy, because the Uri has been changed. Optimizely
      Commerce has no mechanism to detect such changes to
      redirect customers to the new Uri, so don't change your Uri:s
      until absolutely necessary.

         There are third party tools (for example
         https://github.com/Geta/404handler) which can
         handle the redirects if you change your Uri. While
         that works that still hurts your search engine ranking.
         Of course, when you have to (change), you have to.
         Just don't do it very often.

                         How it works

SEO Uri:s are handled by SeoUriRouter, which is

an       implementation  of  ISimpleAddressRouteHandler.

ISimpleAddressRouteHandler has only one method:

1 SimpleAddressRouteValues GetRouteValues(string urlSegment\
2 , ContentReference routeRoot, SegmentContext segmentConte\
3 xt);

SeoUriRouter implementation of this method only handles the
urlSegment without any slash (/) in between - so /this-can-be-
a-seo-uri/ is processed, but /this/cannot/be/a/seo/uri/ is not. It
then try to get the content from the seo uri, and if you are
wondering, there are methods in ICatalogSystem to do that -
GetCatalogEntryByUriDto(string seoUri, string language) and
GetCatalogNodeDto(string seoUri, string language). If a match-
ing content to be found, then a SimpleAddressRouteValues value
Routing  321

will be returned, indicating this router has processed successfully
the urlSegment.

Simple enough, right? However, the design of SeoUriRouter has
some limitations: It's registered by default, and with the routing
system, the first router to be able to "catch" the uri wins, so it's be
a challenge if you want to replace the behavior. We'll look for more
detail in the next section.

              I know, I've been telling you to avoid ICatalogSystem
              as much as possible and embrace the new content
              API:s. This is almost always true. However, there are
              some functions provided by ICatalogSystem which
              are not replaced by new content API:s. In such case,
              you'll have to use ICatalogSystem. It's been improved
              a lot internally anyway.

             The hierarchical approach:

The core concept of this approach is to reflect the structure of
contents to the structure of the Uri, for the discoverability. With
SEO Uri approach, customers will never know how to get to the
parent node, to see the other items in same category. With the
partial routing, they can. To allow that, every catalog content
has a piece of information - RouteSegment, as CatalogContentBase
implements IRoutable.
Routing  322

                    RouteSegment, known as "Name in URL" in Catalog UI

    RouteSegment is also supposed to be unique, but only within
    its siblings. Same as SEO Uri, RouteSegment is generated by
    UniqueSeoGenerator, but by another method:

1 public virtual string GenerateUriSegment(string name, boo\
2 l includeRandomToken)

    It works pretty similar with GenerateSeoUri, except without exten-
    sion part.

                   RouteSegments for category and entry are saved in
                   CatalogItemSeo as UriSegment. RouteSegments of cat-
                   alogs, however, are saved in CatalogLanguage table.

                                  How it works

    HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter implements ICommerceRouter,
    which itself inherit from IPartialRouter<TContent, TRoutedData>,
    IPartialRouter has two methods for incoming and ongoing URL:
Routing  323

1 PartialRouteData GetPartialVirtualPath(TRoutedData conten\

2 t, string language, RouteValueDictionary routeValues, Req\

3 uestContext requestContext);

4 object RoutePartial(TContent content, SegmentContext segm\

5 entContext);

    For incoming URL, the router will parse the URL from the left to
    the right in RoutePartial. The first segment is supposed to match the
    "root content" (not the RootContent, but the configured root) - and
    the next segment is supposed to match the RouteSegment of one of
    the children of the content match the previous segment, recursively.
    If a content matches the entire route, it'll be returned.

    For outgoing, GetPartialVirtualPath will build the URL form right
    to left. Starting with the content is being routed, it'll append the
    RouteSegment of its parent content to the left of the URL, until it
    reach the root content.

    HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter has three important parame-
    ters to pass to its constructor:

        · RouteStartingPoint, which is a Func<ContentReference>.
           It is supposed to return a ContentReference
           to the starting point of the route. If you call

             CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(RouteCollection

           routes, bool enableOutgoingSeoUri), it'll be the
           ContentReference.StartPage (i.e. SiteDefinition.StartPage).
           Because this is a Func<T>, it'll be evaluated every time an
           URL being routed, then it's really dynamic. If you have
           multiple start pages, it can detect the language the customer
           is on, and use the right segment. If you set the Shortcut
           setting for your start page, it can use that, too. Just remember
           to use a fast Func<ContentReference>, otherwise it'll be hit
           bad.
        · CommerceRoot: if you call CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(
           routes, bool enableOutgoingSeoUri), it'll be the "true"
    Routing                                       324

    Commerce Root, but you are not limited by that. You can
    construct an instance of HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter
    which takes a CatalogContentBase and then register it
    directly. This can be done in your initialization module:

1   var referenceConverter = ServiceLocator.Current.GetIn\

2 stance<ReferenceConverter>();

3

4   var contentLoader = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstanc\

5 e<IContentLoader>();

6

7   //Assuming you want to use your only catalog

8   var commerceRootContent = contentLoader.GetChildren<C\

9 atalogContent>(referenceConverter.GetRootLink()).First();

10

11  //RegisterPartialRouter is an extension from Optimize\

12 ly.Web.Routing

13  RouteTables.Routes.RegisterPartialRouter(new Hierarch\

14 icalCatalogPartialRouter(startingPoint, commerceRootConte\

15 nt, enableOutgoingSeoUri));

    This is useful in case you want to hide parts of your catalog
    structure - because it might be sensitive or boring to show it to
    customers. For example, using the default CommerceRoot means
    your catalog RouteSegment will be included in the url, like this:

    http://<your-site>/en/departmental-catalog/Departments/Fashion/

    If you have only on catalog and don't want it to be shown,
    simply point it to your catalog. Your URL will appear to be
    http://<your-site>/en/Departments/Fashion/ only. Of course,
    nothing prevents you from pointing your RouteStartingPoint to a
    category, if you want to. In this example, I can set the Departments
    category to be the RouteStartingPoint and remove it from the
    URL.
Routing  325

· enableOutgoingSeoUri, which is a boolean - if it's true, then
  HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter will work with SEO URI
  instead. Any hierarchical url which matches a catalog content
  will be redirected to its SEO URI.

                            Redirecting

    A very good thing about Optimizely Commerce is that even you
    enabled the partial routing system, the old SEO Uri:s will continue
    to work (of course, as long as you keep the Uri:s unchanged). So
    they can coexist in same website - but that might not what you
    want - you might want to stick with only one routing system - it's
    been told that the more popular your URL is, the higher rank it gets
    in the search engine results.

                   From hierarchical URL to SEO URI

    This one is pretty easy, just call this during your site's initialization,
    the true parameter means you want to enable Outgoing Seo URI,
    and the next statement configures the redirect:

1 CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(RouteTable\
2 .Routes, true);
3 CatalogRouteHelper.SetupSeoUriPermanentRedirect();

               From SEO URI to hierarchial URI:

This one is tricky, there is no such built-in method allows us to
do so. We can't even override SeoUriRouter because it's registered
automatically and there is no way to guarantee that our router
will be able to run before it. (If a matching router is found, the
processing will stop). Let's explore other options.

EPiServer.Web.Routing.ContentRoute has two events which might
be interesting - RoutingContent and RoutedContent. As their names
Routing  326

might suggest, the former is fired before any routers take place,
while the latter is fired after that. We might not want to listen
to RoutingContent because it's too early, we'll have to process all
URI:s sent to our site, which will slow it down. It's better to only
process after the URI has been routed successfully, preferably by
SeoUriRouter. We can do some checks and redirect if necessary.

First, we need to register to RoutedContent event:

1 ContentRoute.RoutedContent += Routed_SeoUri;

    And then implement it. It's another void method which
    takes an object as sender and an RoutingEventArgs as the
    EventArgs. RoutingEventArgs has SegmentContext property named
    RoutingSegmentContext, this is what we need. A successfully
    routed route will set the RouteObject of RoutingSegmentContext
    to the content it found. So we can check if that content is a catalog
    content or not, to see if we should redirect.

    But how can we know if the URI was SEO URI, or hierarchical
    URI? By checking for slash in the between? Not really effective,
    because the top level content only has one segment. We can mimic
    SeoUriRouter and try to load t `he Entry/Node by Uri? Yes that
    might work but it is not really fast. If we are lucky and the URI
    was SEO URI, the DTO should have been cached, but if it was
    hierarchical URI, we will have to hit the database to find nothing!

    Solution, well, HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter will process to
    the last segment, and for each segment, the LastConsumedFragment
    is assigned to next segment, so it'll be empty at the end.
    SeoUriRouter, in other hand, does not really process any segment,
    so the LastConsumedFragment it returns will be the same path as
    requested.

    With that information, we can have this as our method:
    Routing                                               327

1 private static void Routed_SeoUri(object sender, RoutingE\

2 ventArgs e)

3{

4     var context = e.RoutingSegmentContext;

5     //RoutedObject is supposed to not be null here

6     if (!(context.RoutedObject is CatalogContentBase))

7     {

8            return;

9     }

10

11    if(string.IsNullOrEmpty(context.LastConsumedFragment))

12    {

13           return;

14    }

15    var urlResolver = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<\

16 UrlResolver>();

17    context.PermanentRedirect(urlResolver.GetUrl(context.\

18 RoutedContentLink));

19 }

    And now we can redirect the SEO URI to Hierarchical URI in an
    effective way.
             Settings

There are many settings that you can be used to tune things in
Optimizely Customized Commerce (and the underlying Optimizely
Content Cloud as well). Sadly, there is no central document of
which settings are available, what they can be used for, what is
the default setting, recommended setting (if any), and impacts it
might have. In most cases you will have to dig it up, or probably,
asking developer support service if there is any setting you can use.

In general, I advise to avoid changing the cache settings unless you
absolutely want to (and know what you are doing). It can be more
relaxed toward other settings, but make sure you know what it is
doing (and any possible side effects).
            Appendix

How to ensure a successful upgrade.

I, as an Optimizely employee, always recommend our customers
to upgrade to the latest version possible. Well, of course that is
constrained by a number of things - time (you might be in a
deadline next Tuesday), money (upgrading might mean license
renewal and that costs money. I'm not allowed to discuss about
the licensing, but one thing for sure it's not free), uncertainty (our
system is working, why upgrade), etc. But if you can, you always
should:

    · Newer version is almost always better. More features. More
      bugs fixed. More performance. Period. Try to import your
      catalog in Commerce 8. Then try with Commerce 9. It's not
      even close.

    · Fresh code is better. Says, you have on problem and you ask
      Optimizely developers for help in World forums. They (me
      and my colleagues) will try to help you the best they can, but
      they need to understand the problem first. If you are in very
      recent version, they can fire up Visual Studio, look at the code
      and say "OK, it works this way". But if you're in a very old
      version, it'll be like this "Anyone remember how did it work
      in <dinosaur> version. I think we changed it last time, right?
      No one is remember?" and then they will have to dig through
      a very long history, just to find an answer. You can wait?

    · Bug fixes come sooner. Optimizely developers don't fix bug,
      they write perfect code. No, I lied. Despite their best effort to
      avoid bugs and QA's best effort to catch bugs, it still happens,
      sometimes. If you have a bug in a very recent version, it'll be
Appendix  330

      easier to fix and you can get the fixed version in 2-3 weeks. If
      you were in last major version, or even older, there will be no
      hotfixes for you. And in the end you'll have to upgrade. Why
      not stay ahead in your game?

Of course, upgrading is not an hassle-free task. (But it has been
much more easier in recent years). If you have gone through all the
way to upgrade your site from Commerce R1/R2 to latest version,
then Congratulations! Next time you're in Stockholm, I will buy
you one beer or two. If you are planning to do that, here's something
that might help you:

    · Backing up is the key. Always back up your site and your
      database before you start. I know you've done it, but I never
      feel that I can urge this enough.

    · It's better to work with small steps than a big one. You
      will have more control. (If you play games, you will know
      why this is so important. It's better to replay the last 15
      minutes because you made a wrong decision, than throwing
      your controller because you would have to replay the entire
      day). Always upgrade to next major version. (And in term
      of major version, Commerce 7.5 counts as one). Upgrade,
      check if everything, including basic features in both front-
      end and back-end sites work normally. Back up your sites
      and databases. Then continue. You can try to upgrade to 2-
      3 major versions above, and it might work but I would not
      recommend that. When something happens (and it always
      happens), you won't know what went wrong.

    · In most cases, you should upgrade your front-end site first.
      Database changes are done there. Migration Steps are done
      there. When everything is good, you're free to upgrade your
      Commerce Manager. In fact, unless you have custom changes
      made to your Commerce Manager, you can simply create
      a new Commerce Manager site using the matching version
      with your front-end site, then point your connection strings
Appendix  331

  to your database, should your Commerce Manager upgrade
  run into problems.

· Watch the migration steps closely. All of them should be run
  successfully before you start using your sites. Note that from
  Commerce 8.16, Migration steps have been mandatory, you
  can't no longer, intentionally or accidentally prevent them
  from starting. (You can technically skip steps if you really,
  really want to, but I highly recommend against)

· In many cases, you can (and sometimes, have to) increase
  the database connection time out to let the migration steps
  complete. As some of them do a very heavy migrating jobs,
  they might exceed the default 30 seconds limit for connection
  time.

· If you upgrade from an older version where catalog content
  versions do not exist (Commerce R3a or earlier), it does make
  sense to export your catalogs before upgrading, then import
  them after upgrading. Truth is, the catalog import/export is
  much more robust than upgrade scripts and migration steps.
  This can also apply for newer versions, if you do not care for
  draft versions and such.
